<!-- question-1: body_md -->
Can one instead find a sequence of smooth projective geometrically
connected curves $C_n/\mathbb Q$ with $g(C_n)\to\infty$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-statement-1}
 \log\mathfrak N(C_n)=O(g(C_n))?
\end{equation}
<!-- end question-1 -->
<!-- question-2: body_md -->
Does there exist a regular integral quasi-projective arithmetic surface
$S\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ with a section and infinite \'etale fundamental group?
<!-- end question-2 -->
<!-- question-3: body_md -->
Does there exist a finitely generated field $k$ of characteristic zero,
a smooth projective geometrically connected curve $C/k$, and a point
$P\in C(k)$ admitting geometrically connected finite \'etale Galois
covers of unbounded degree in which $P$ splits completely?
<!-- end question-3 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-surface}
There exist a regular projective arithmetic surface $S$ over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$,
a section $s:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\to S$, and a tower
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-statement-4}
\cdots\longrightarrow S_{n+1}\longrightarrow S_n\longrightarrow\cdots
\longrightarrow S_0=S
\end{equation}
with the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Each $S_n\to S$ is a connected finite \'etale Galois cover of
degree $2^n$, and each transition map $S_{n+1}\to S_n$ has degree two.
\item Every $S_n$ is a regular projective arithmetic surface with
smooth geometrically connected generic fibre.
\item The section $s$ splits completely at every level:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-statement-5}
S_n\times_{S,s}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\simeq\coprod_{j=1}^{2^n}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
In particular, $\pi_1^{\mathrm{geom}}(S)=\pi_1^{\mathrm{\acute et}}(S)$ is infinite.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-curves}
For every sufficiently large integer $g$, there are a smooth projective
geometrically connected curve $C/\mathbb Q$ of genus $g$, a point $P\in C(\mathbb Q)$,
and a tower of smooth projective geometrically connected curves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-statement-6}
 \cdots\longrightarrow D_{n+1}\longrightarrow D_n
 \longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow D_0=C
\end{equation}
such that $D_n\to C$ is finite \'etale Galois of degree $2^n$ and $P$
splits completely in every $D_n$. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-statement-7}
 g(D_n)-1=2^n(g-1),\qquad \#D_n(\mathbb Q)\ge2^n,
\end{equation}
and the $\mathbb Q$-rational geometric fundamental group of $(C,P)$ is infinite.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-conductor}
There exist a constant $A>0$ and a sequence of smooth projective
geometrically connected curves $C_n/\mathbb Q$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-statement-10}
 g(C_n)\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 \log\mathfrak N(C_n)\le A g(C_n)\quad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
The curves can be chosen in a tower with finite \'etale Galois maps
$C_n\to C_0$ of degree $2^n$, in which a point of $C_0(\mathbb Q)$ splits
completely at every level.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:bg-normal-crossings}
Let $X$ be a regular surface. A reduced effective divisor $D\subset X$
has strict normal crossings if, for every closed point $x\in D$, there
is a regular system of parameters $(t_1,t_2)$ of $\mathcal O_{X,x}$
such that the ideal of $D$ in $\mathcal O_{X,x}$ is either $(t_1)$ or
$(t_1t_2)$. In the second case, the two components through $x$ have
local ideals $(t_1)$ and $(t_2)$; we say that the components meet
transversely at $x$. A divisor has normal crossings if the same
condition holds after replacing $X$ by an \'etale neighbourhood of
each point (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0CBN}{\textup{[Stacks, 0CBN]}}).
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- backgroundproposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:conductor-model-transport}
Let $R$ be an excellent discrete valuation ring with fraction field $F$,
and let $X/R$ be proper with smooth geometrically connected generic fibre
$D/F$. Let $Y\to X$ be finite \'etale with geometrically connected
generic fibre $D'$. If $W/R$ is a regular proper model of $D$, then the
normalization $W'$ of $W$ in $F(D')$ is finite \'etale over $W$.
<!-- end backgroundproposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The finite \'etale morphism $D'\to D$ identifies $F(D')$ as a finite
separable extension of the function field $F(D)$ of $W$. Since $W$ is
excellent, its normalization $\nu:W'\to W$ in $F(D')$ is finite.
Let $w$ be a codimension-one point of $W$. The local ring
$B=\mathcal O_{W,w}$ is a discrete valuation ring with fraction field
$F(D)$. The identification $W_F\simeq D=X_F$ gives an $R$-morphism
$j:\operatorname{Spec} F(D)\to X$. Since $X\to\operatorname{Spec} R$ is proper, the valuative
criterion gives an $R$-morphism $j_B:\operatorname{Spec} B\to X$ satisfying
$j_B|_{\operatorname{Spec} F(D)}=j$ (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0BX5}{\textup{[Stacks, 0BX5]}}).

Write $Y\times_X\operatorname{Spec} B=\operatorname{Spec} A_B$. The $B$-algebra $A_B$ is finite
\'etale and has generic algebra
$A_B\otimes_BF(D)=F(D')$. Since $B$ is regular and $A_B$ is
\'etale over $B$, the ring $A_B$ is regular and therefore normal.
Since $A_B$ is torsion-free over $B$, the
map $A_B\to A_B\otimes_BF(D)=F(D')$ is injective. Since $A_B$ is
finite over $B$, every element of $A_B$ is integral over $B$.
Conversely, if $a\in F(D')$ is integral over $B$, then $a$ is
integral over $A_B$, so $a\in A_B$ because $A_B$ is normal. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-transport-integral-closure}
 A_B=\overline B^{\,F(D')},\qquad
 W'\times_W\operatorname{Spec} B\simeq\operatorname{Spec} A_B.
\end{equation}
The morphism $\operatorname{Spec} A_B\to\operatorname{Spec} B$ is \'etale. Therefore
$\nu:W'\to W$ is \'etale at every point of $\nu^{-1}(w)$, for
every codimension-one point $w\in W$.

The scheme $W'$ is normal and $W$ is regular. Choose an affine open
$\operatorname{Spec} A\subset W$ and write $\nu^{-1}(\operatorname{Spec} A)=\operatorname{Spec} A'$, where
$A\subset A'$ is a finite extension of normal domains. For
$w'\in\operatorname{Spec} A'$ and $w=\nu(w')$, the going-down theorem and
incomparability for primes in $A\subset A'$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-transport-local-dimensions}
 \dim\mathcal O_{W',w'}=\dim\mathcal O_{W,w}
\end{equation}
(\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/00ON}{\textup{[Stacks, 00ON]}}). The morphism $\nu:W'\to W$ is generically \'etale
because $D'\to D$ is
\'etale. Purity of the branch locus (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0BMB}{\textup{[Stacks, 0BMB]}}) therefore shows that
$\nu:W'\to W$ is \'etale everywhere.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- backgroundproposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:conductor-common-extension}
Let $F$ be a finite extension of $\mathbb Q_p$, and let $X/\mathcal O_F$ be
proper with smooth geometrically connected generic curve $D/F$.
There is a finite Galois extension $E/F$ and a regular semistable model
$W/\mathcal O_E$ of $D_E$ with the following property. For every finite
\'etale cover $Y\to X$ with geometrically connected generic curve $D'$,
the normalization $W'$ of $W$ in the function field $E(D'_E)$ is finite
\'etale over $W$ and is a regular semistable
model of $D'_E$.
<!-- end backgroundproposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Semistable reduction for curves (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0CDN}{\textup{[Stacks, 0CDN]}}) gives a finite extension
$E_0/F$ and a semistable model $M_0/\mathcal O_{E_0}$ of $D_{E_0}$.
Let $E/F$ be the Galois closure of $E_0/F$. The base change
$M=M_0\times_{\mathcal O_{E_0}}\mathcal O_E$ is a nodal proper model
of $D_E$. Apply the recursion
\eqref{eq:bg-nodal-resolution-sequence} with initial model $M$.
Let $N$ be the first index for which $Z_N=\varnothing$, and put
$W=M_N$. By \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0CDE}{\textup{[Stacks, 0CDE]}}, the scheme $W$ is regular and
$W_{\overline k}$ is reduced and nodal, where $k$ is the residue
field of $E$. Thus $W/\mathcal O_E$ is a regular semistable model
of $D_E$.

Apply Proposition~\ref{prop:conductor-model-transport} to
$X_{\mathcal O_E}=X\times_{\mathcal O_F}\mathcal O_E$, the cover
$Y_{\mathcal O_E}\to X_{\mathcal O_E}$ and the regular model $W$.
The proposition gives a finite \'etale morphism $\nu:W'\to W$,
where $W'$ is the normalization of $W$ in $E(D'_E)$. Since $W$ is
regular and $\nu$ is \'etale, $W'$ is regular. Since $\nu$ is finite
and flat and $W\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_E$ is proper and flat,
$W'\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_E$ is proper and flat. The induced morphism
$W'_{\overline k}\to W_{\overline k}$ is finite \'etale. Since
$W_{\overline k}$ is reduced and nodal, $W'_{\overline k}$ is
reduced and nodal. Normalization commutes with restriction to
$W_E=D_E$, so $(W')_E$ is the normalization of $D_E$ in $E(D'_E)$.
The finite morphism $D'_E\to D_E$ has normal source and induces the
extension $E(D'_E)/E(D_E)$; hence $(W')_E\simeq D'_E$.
Thus $W'/\mathcal O_E$ is a regular semistable model of $D'_E$.
The choices of $E$ and $W$ depend on $D$, independently of the
cover $Y\to X$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- backgroundproposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:conductor-local-bound}
Let $D/F$ be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve over a
finite extension of $\mathbb Q_p$. Suppose $D$ acquires semistable reduction
over a finite Galois extension $E/F$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-explicit-last-break}
 b(E/F)=\sup\bigl(\{0\}\cup
       \{u>0:\operatorname{Gal}(E/F)^u\ne1\}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Then the integer $a_F(D)$ defined in
\eqref{eq:conductor-artin-swan} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-explicit-local-bound}
 a_F(D)\le 2\bigl(1+b(E/F)\bigr)g(D).
\end{equation}
<!-- end backgroundproposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Fix an algebraic closure $\overline F$ containing $E$. Put
$b=b(E/F)$ as in \eqref{eq:bg-explicit-last-break} and
$V=H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(D_{\overline F},\mathbb Q_q)$ for $q\ne p$.
For $u>b$, compatibility of upper numbering with the quotient map
$G_F\to\operatorname{Gal}(E/F)$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-local-bound-kernel}
 G_F^u\subseteq\ker\bigl(G_F\to\operatorname{Gal}(E/F)\bigr)=G_E.
\end{equation}
Since $u>0$, the group $G_F^u$ acts trivially on the algebraic
closure of the residue field. Hence $G_F^u\subseteq I_E$.
The group $G_F^u$ is pro-$p$, and every finite quotient of
$I_E/P_E$ has order prime to $p$. Therefore the homomorphism
$G_F^u\to I_E/P_E$ is trivial and $G_F^u\subseteq P_E$.
Semistable monodromy for $D_E$ gives $V^{P_E}=V$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-local-bound-invariants}
 V^{G_F^u}=V\qquad(u>b).
\end{equation}
For $0<u\leq b$, we have
$\dim_{\mathbb Q_q}(V/V^{G_F^u})\leq\dim_{\mathbb Q_q}V=2g(D)$.
Using \eqref{eq:bg-local-bound-invariants} in
\eqref{eq:conductor-artin-swan}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-local-bound-integral}
 \operatorname{Sw}_F(V)
 =\int_0^b\dim_{\mathbb Q_q}(V/V^{G_F^u})\,du
 \leq2b\,g(D).
\end{equation}
Finally,
$a_F(D)=\dim_{\mathbb Q_q}(V/V^{I_F})+\operatorname{Sw}_F(V)
\leq2g(D)+2b\,g(D)=2(1+b)g(D)$, as claimed in
\eqref{eq:bg-explicit-local-bound}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- backgroundproposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:conductor-uniform-integral-covers}
Let $S/\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ be a proper flat arithmetic surface with smooth
geometrically connected generic curve $C/\mathbb Q$. Let $\Sigma$ be a finite
set of primes such that $S$ is smooth over
$U=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z[1/\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p]$.
For each $p\in\Sigma$, let $E_p/\mathbb Q_p$ be a finite Galois extension
over which $C$ has semistable reduction, and put $b_p=b(E_p/\mathbb Q_p)$.
Then every finite \'etale cover $T\to S$ with geometrically connected
generic curve $D/\mathbb Q$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-explicit-uniform-bound}
 a_p(D)=0\quad(p\notin\Sigma),\qquad
 \log\mathfrak N(D)\le A(S,\Sigma)g(D),
 \quad
 A(S,\Sigma)=2\sum_{p\in\Sigma}(1+b_p)\log p.
\end{equation}
In particular, the constant $A(S,\Sigma)$ in
\eqref{eq:bg-explicit-uniform-bound} is independent of the degree of $T\to S$.
<!-- end backgroundproposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The composition $T_U\to S_U\to U$ is smooth and proper because
$T_U\to S_U$ is finite \'etale and $S_U\to U$ is smooth and proper.
For every $p\notin\Sigma$ and $q\ne p$, smooth proper base change
(\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/095T}{\textup{[Stacks, 095T]}}, \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0GLI}{\textup{[Stacks, 0GLI]}}) implies that $I_{\mathbb Q_p}$ acts trivially on
$H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(D_{\overline\mathbb Q_p},\mathbb Q_q)$, so $a_p(D)=0$.

For $p\in\Sigma$, choose a regular semistable model
$W_p/\mathcal O_{E_p}$ of $C_{E_p}$. Apply
Proposition~\ref{prop:conductor-model-transport} to
$S_{\mathcal O_{E_p}}$, its finite \'etale cover
$T_{\mathcal O_{E_p}}$ and $W_p$. The proposition gives a finite
\'etale morphism $W'_p\to W_p$, where $W'_p$ is the normalization
of $W_p$ in $E_p(D_{E_p})$. Write $k_p$ for the residue field of
$E_p$. Since $W_p$ is regular and
the morphism $W'_p\to W_p$ is \'etale, $W'_p$ is regular.
The base change
$(W'_p)_{\overline{k_p}}\to(W_p)_{\overline{k_p}}$ is finite
\'etale, and $(W_p)_{\overline{k_p}}$ is reduced and nodal.
Hence $(W'_p)_{\overline{k_p}}$ is reduced and nodal. Moreover,
$(W'_p)_{E_p}\simeq D_{E_p}$ and $W'_p\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{E_p}$
is proper and flat. Thus $D_{E_p}$ has semistable reduction.
Proposition~\ref{prop:conductor-local-bound} gives
$a_p(D)\leq2(1+b_p)g(D)$ for every $p\in\Sigma$. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bg-uniform-conductor-sum}
 \log\mathfrak N(D)
 =\sum_{p\in\Sigma}a_p(D)\log p
 \leq2g(D)\sum_{p\in\Sigma}(1+b_p)\log p
 =A(S,\Sigma)g(D),
\end{equation}
as claimed in \eqref{eq:bg-explicit-uniform-bound}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:genus-spreading}
Let $C/\mathbb Q$ be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus
$b\ge2$, and let $P\in C(\mathbb Q)$. For every integer $h\ge3b$ there are a
smooth projective geometrically connected curve $D/\mathbb Q$ of genus $h$
and a ramified double cover $f:D\to C$ in which $P$ splits completely.
For every geometrically connected finite \'etale Galois cover $C'\to C$,
the pullback $D\times_C C'\to D$ is geometrically connected and has the
same Galois group and degree as $C'\to C$. If $P$ splits completely in
$C'\to C$, each of the two rational points in $f^{-1}(P)$ splits
completely in $D\times_C C'\to D$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Put $k=h-2b+1\ge b+1$ and $L=\mathcal O_C(kP)$, and choose an
identification $L|_P\simeq\mathbb Q$. Use the induced identification
$L^{\otimes2}|_P\simeq\mathbb Q$ to define the value $s(P)$ of a section
$s\in H^0(C,L^{\otimes2})$.
For a line bundle $M$ on $C$ with $\deg M>2b-2$, Serre duality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-duality}
 H^1(C,M)\simeq H^0(C,\omega_C\otimes M^{-1})^\vee=0,
\end{equation}
since $\deg(\omega_C\otimes M^{-1})<0$; see \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0E39}{\textup{[Stacks, 0E39]}}.
For every field extension $E/\mathbb Q$, the inequality
$\deg(M_E)=\deg M>2b-2$ and Serre duality give $H^1(C_E,M_E)=0$.
Applying \eqref{eq:genus-explicit-duality} to $L^{\otimes2}(-P)$ and
then taking cohomology of
$0\to L^{\otimes2}(-P)\to L^{\otimes2}\to L^{\otimes2}|_P\to0$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-evaluation}
 H^1(C,L^{\otimes2}(-P))=0,\qquad
 H^0(C,L^{\otimes2})\twoheadrightarrow L^{\otimes2}|_P.
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-sections}
 V=\{s\in H^0(C,L^{\otimes2}):s(P)=1\}
\end{equation}
is a nonempty affine space over $\mathbb Q$.

Write $\overline C=C_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ and
$\overline L=L|_{\overline C}$.
For $x\in\overline C(\overline\mathbb Q)$ and a local generator $e$
of $\overline L^{\otimes2}$ at $x$, write $s=ae$ and define
$\operatorname{ord}_x(s)=v_x(a)$.
Replacing $e$ by $ue$, with $u\in\mathcal O_{\overline C,x}^{\times}$,
replaces $a$ by $u^{-1}a$, so this definition does not depend on $e$.
We will choose $s\in V(\mathbb Q)$ such that
$\operatorname{ord}_x(s)=1$ at every zero
$x\in\overline C(\overline\mathbb Q)$ of $s$.
For $x\in\overline C(\overline\mathbb Q)\setminus\{P\}$, let
$2x\subset\overline C$ be the closed subscheme defined by the ideal
sheaf $\mathcal O_{\overline C}(-2x)$; the local ring of $2x$ at $x$ is
$\mathcal O_{\overline C,x}/\mathfrak m_x^2$ and has length two.
The inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-restriction-degree}
 \deg(\overline L^{\otimes2}(-P-2x))=2k-3>2b-2
\end{equation}
and Serre duality give
$H^1(\overline C,\overline L^{\otimes2}(-P-2x))=0$.
The restriction sequence therefore gives a surjection
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-restriction-map}
 H^0(\overline C,\overline L^{\otimes2}(-P))
 \longrightarrow H^0(2x,\overline L^{\otimes2}|_{2x}).
\end{equation}
The vector space on the right of
\eqref{eq:genus-explicit-restriction-map} has dimension two over
$\overline\mathbb Q$. Fix $s_0\in V(\mathbb Q)$. Every element of
$V(\overline\mathbb Q)$ has a unique expression $s_0+u$, with
$u\in H^0(\overline C,\overline L^{\otimes2}(-P))$.
The condition $\operatorname{ord}_x(s_0+u)\ge2$ is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-double-zero}
 u|_{2x}=-s_0|_{2x}.
\end{equation}
Surjectivity in \eqref{eq:genus-explicit-restriction-map} implies
that the set of solutions of \eqref{eq:genus-explicit-double-zero}
is an affine subspace of $V_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ of codimension two.

We next construct a closed subscheme $Z\subset V\times C$ whose
geometric points satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-incidence-geometric-points}
 Z(\overline\mathbb Q)=
 \{(s,x)\in V(\overline\mathbb Q)\times\overline C(\overline\mathbb Q):
                       \operatorname{ord}_x(s)\ge2\}.
\end{equation}
Choose a basis $s_1,\ldots,s_r$ of
$H^0(C,L^{\otimes2}(-P))$, and give $V$ coordinates
$a_1,\ldots,a_r$ by the equality $s=s_0+\sum_{j=1}^r a_js_j$.
Choose an affine open covering $(W_\alpha)_\alpha$ of $C$ such that
each $L^{\otimes2}|_{W_\alpha}$ has a generator $e_\alpha$ and
each $W_\alpha$ admits an \'etale morphism
$t_\alpha:W_\alpha\to\mathbb A^1_\mathbb Q$.
For $0\le j\le r$, define $f_{j,\alpha}\in\mathcal O_C(W_\alpha)$
by $s_j|_{W_\alpha}=f_{j,\alpha}e_\alpha$.
For a regular function $a$ on $W_\alpha$, define
$\partial a/\partial t_\alpha$ by
$da=(\partial a/\partial t_\alpha)\,dt_\alpha$.
Define the function $F_\alpha$ and the ideal sheaf
$\mathcal I_\alpha$ on $V\times W_\alpha$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-incidence-equations}
 F_\alpha=f_{0,\alpha}+\sum_{j=1}^r a_jf_{j,\alpha},\qquad
 \mathcal I_\alpha=
 \left(F_\alpha,\frac{\partial F_\alpha}{\partial t_\alpha}\right)
 \mathcal O_{V\times W_\alpha}.
\end{equation}
For $W_{\alpha\beta}=W_\alpha\cap W_\beta$, define units
$\lambda_{\alpha\beta},c_{\alpha\beta}$ on $W_{\alpha\beta}$ by
$e_\alpha=\lambda_{\alpha\beta}e_\beta$ and
$dt_\beta=c_{\alpha\beta}\,dt_\alpha$.
Restriction to $V\times W_{\alpha\beta}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-gluing}
 \begin{aligned}
 F_\beta&=\lambda_{\alpha\beta}F_\alpha,\\
 \frac{\partial F_\beta}{\partial t_\beta}
 &=c_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}\lambda_{\alpha\beta}
       \frac{\partial F_\alpha}{\partial t_\alpha}
   +c_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}
       \frac{\partial\lambda_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial t_\alpha}
       F_\alpha,\\
 \mathcal I_\beta|_{V\times W_{\alpha\beta}}
 &=\mathcal I_\alpha|_{V\times W_{\alpha\beta}}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The ideal sheaves $\mathcal I_\alpha$ therefore define an ideal
sheaf $\mathcal I\subset\mathcal O_{V\times C}$. Define $Z$ to be
the closed subscheme of $V\times C$ with ideal sheaf $\mathcal I$.
A geometric point $(s,x)$ belongs to $Z$ if and only if the constant
and linear coefficients of $s$ in a local parameter at $x$ are zero.
This proves the asserted description of $Z(\overline\mathbb Q)$.

Let $p_C:Z\to C$ and $p_V:Z\to V$ be the restrictions of the
projections $(s,x)\mapsto x$ and $(s,x)\mapsto s$, respectively.
Since $s(P)=1$ for every $s\in V$, the fibre $p_C^{-1}(P)$ is empty.
For $x\in\overline C(\overline\mathbb Q)\setminus\{P\}$, equation
\eqref{eq:genus-explicit-double-zero} identifies $p_C^{-1}(x)$
with an affine space of dimension $r-2$. The dimension bound for
the fibres of $p_C$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-incidence-dimension}
 \dim Z\le\dim C+r-2=\dim V-1.
\end{equation}
The morphism $p_V$ is proper, because $Z$ is closed in $V\times C$
and $C$ is proper over $\mathbb Q$. Hence $p_V(Z)$ is closed in $V$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-image-dimension}
 \dim p_V(Z)\le\dim Z\le\dim V-1.
\end{equation}
The nonempty open subset $V\setminus p_V(Z)$ of the affine space
$V$ contains a rational point. Choose
$s\in(V\setminus p_V(Z))(\mathbb Q)$. Then $s(P)=1$ and
$\operatorname{ord}_x(s)=1$ at every geometric zero $x$ of $s$.

The section $s$ defines the $\mathcal O_C$-linear map
$\mu_s:L^{-2}\to\mathcal O_C$ that sends $e^{-2}$ to $a_W$
whenever $e$ generates $L|_W$ and $s|_W=a_We^2$.
Make $\mathcal B=\mathcal O_C\oplus L^{-1}$ an
$\mathcal O_C$-algebra by the multiplication rule
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-algebra-product}
 (a,u)(b,v)=(ab+\mu_s(u\otimes v),\,av+bu).
\end{equation}
Define the curve $D$ and the morphism $f$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-relative-spectrum}
 D=\operatorname{Spec}_C(\mathcal B),\qquad f:D\longrightarrow C.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal B$ is locally free of rank two over $\mathcal O_C$,
the morphism $f$ is finite locally free of degree two.
For an affine open $W=\operatorname{Spec} A\subset C$ with a generator $e$
of $L|_W$, write $s|_W=a_We^2$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-affine-cover}
 f^{-1}(W)=\operatorname{Spec} A[w]/(w^2-a_W).
\end{equation}
On $f^{-1}(\{a_W\ne0\})$, the element $w$ is a unit and
$\partial(w^2-a_W)/\partial w=2w$ is a unit. Consequently, $f$ is
\'etale on $f^{-1}(\{a_W\ne0\})$, and $D$ is smooth over $\mathbb Q$
on that open set.
At a geometric zero $x$ of $a_W$, choose a local parameter $t$ on
$\overline C$ at $x$. The equality
$\operatorname{ord}_x(a_W)=1$ gives
$(\partial a_W/\partial t)(x)\ne0$. Hence
$\partial(w^2-a_W)/\partial t\ne0$ at the point $(x,0)$ of $D$,
so $D$ is smooth at $(x,0)$.
In the local ring of $D$ at $(x,0)$, the element $w$ is a
uniformizer and $a_W=w^2$. Thus $f$ has ramification index two at
$(x,0)$. It follows that the ramification points of $f$ are exactly
the points above the zeros of $s$. The number of geometric zeros
of $s$ is $\deg L^{\otimes2}=2k$.

Choose a rational generator $e$ of $\overline L$ and write $s=ae^2$,
with $a\in\overline\mathbb Q(C)^\times$. At a zero $x$ of $s$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-odd-valuation}
 v_x(a)=1-2\operatorname{ord}_x(e)\equiv1\pmod2.
\end{equation}
Therefore $a\notin\overline\mathbb Q(C)^{\times2}$ and
$\overline\mathbb Q(C)[w]/(w^2-a)$ is a field.
Every connected component of $D_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ maps onto
$\overline C$: the restriction of the finite flat map $f$ to a
connected component has nonempty open and closed image.
Since the generic fibre of $f_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ is the spectrum of
one field, $D_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ has one connected component.
Thus $D$ is geometrically connected.
The curve $D$ is projective, since $f$ is finite and $C$ is projective.
By $s(P)=1$, the fibre of $f$ above $P$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-point-fibre}
 D\times_{C,P}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q[w]/(w^2-1)
 \simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q\amalg\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q.
\end{equation}
The Riemann--Hurwitz formula for $f$ (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0C1B}{\textup{[Stacks, 0C1B]}}) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-riemann-hurwitz}
 2g(D)-2=2(2b-2)+2k,\qquad g(D)=2b-1+k=h.
\end{equation}

Let $C'\to C$ be as in the statement. Put
$K=\overline\mathbb Q(C)$, $M=\overline\mathbb Q(D)$ and
$M'=\overline\mathbb Q(C')$, viewed as subfields of an algebraic closure of
$K$. The extension $M/K$ is ramified, whereas $M'/K$ is unramified
at every point of $\overline C$. Hence $M\not\subseteq M'$.
Since $[M:K]=2$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-disjointness}
 M\cap M'=K,\qquad [MM':M']=[M:K]=2.
\end{equation}
Multiplication induces an isomorphism of $K$-algebras
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-explicit-generic-tensor}
 M\otimes_K M'\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }MM',\qquad
 a\otimes b\longmapsto ab.
\end{equation}
Put $T=(D\times_C C')_{\overline\mathbb Q}$, and let
$p_D:T\to D_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ be the morphism induced by
$(d,c')\mapsto d$. The morphism $p_D$ is finite \'etale, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-tensor-generic-fibre}
 T\times_{D_{\overline\mathbb Q}}\operatorname{Spec} M=\operatorname{Spec}(M\otimes_K M')
                                      =\operatorname{Spec} MM'.
\end{equation}
For each connected component $T_j\subset T$, the restriction
$p_D|_{T_j}$ is finite \'etale. Thus $p_D(T_j)$ is nonempty, open
and closed in the connected curve $D_{\overline\mathbb Q}$, and
$p_D(T_j)=D_{\overline\mathbb Q}$. Every $T_j$ therefore contains a point
above the generic point of $D_{\overline\mathbb Q}$.
Since $\operatorname{Spec} MM'$ consists of one point, $T$ has one connected
component. This proves that $D\times_C C'$ is geometrically connected.
Write $\pi_D:D\times_C C'\to D$ for the projection and put
$d=\deg(C'\to C)$. Base change gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-pullback-degree-group}
 \deg\pi_D=d,\qquad
 \operatorname{Aut}_D(D\times_C C')\simeq\operatorname{Aut}_C(C').
\end{equation}
Let $Q_1,Q_2\in D(\mathbb Q)$ be the points with $f(Q_j)=P$ from
\eqref{eq:genus-explicit-point-fibre}. If $P$ splits completely in
$C'\to C$, then, for $j=1,2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genus-pullback-split-fibres}
 (D\times_C C')\times_{D,Q_j}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q
 \simeq C'\times_{C,P}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q
 \simeq\coprod_{a=1}^{d}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q,
\end{equation}
as desired.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-curves}
The curves $C_{-1}$ and $C_0$ are smooth, projective and geometrically
integral over $\mathbb Q$. The degrees of the morphisms to
$\mathbb P^1_\mathbb Q$ are
$\deg\rho_{-1}=2$ and $\deg\rho_0=2^{g+1}$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Let $k$ be an algebraic closure of $\mathbb Q$.
For a nonempty subset $I\subseteq\{0,\ldots,g\}$ and $j\in I$,
the valuation at $x=j$ satisfies
$v_{x=j}(\prod_{i\in I}f_i)=1$. Hence
$\prod_{i\in I}f_i\notin k(x)^{\times2}$.
Thus the classes of $f_0,\ldots,f_g$ in
$k(x)^\times/k(x)^{\times2}$ are linearly independent, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-geometric-degree}
 [k(x)(\sqrt{f_0},\ldots,\sqrt{f_g}):k(x)]=2^{g+1}.
\end{equation}
The $k[x]$-module $A_{0,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ is free with basis
$\{\prod_{i=0}^g z_i^{e_i}:e_i\in\{0,1\}\}$.
Localization therefore gives an injective homomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-geometric-ring-injection}
 A_{0,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
 (A_{0,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k)\otimes_{k[x]}k(x)
       =k(x)(\sqrt{f_0},\ldots,\sqrt{f_g}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, $A_{0,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ is a domain.
The $k[t]$-module $A_{0,t}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ is free with basis
$\{\prod_{i=0}^g v_i^{e_i}:e_i\in\{0,1\}\}$.
The isomorphism $\beta_0$ in \eqref{eq:start-explicit-cover-overlap}
identifies the localization of $A_{0,t}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ at
$k[t]\setminus\{0\}$ with the field
in \eqref{eq:start-explicit-geometric-degree}.
Thus $A_{0,t}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ is also a domain. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-cover-open-intersection}
 (U_{0,x})_k\cap(U_{0,t})_k
 =\operatorname{Spec}((A_{0,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k)[x^{-1}])\ne\varnothing,
\end{equation}
because $x$ is a nonzero element of the domain
$A_{0,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$. This proves that $(C_0)_k$ is integral.

The equality $v_{x=0}(F)=1$ gives $F\notin k(x)^{\times2}$.
The free rank-two modules $A_{-1,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ over $k[x]$
and $A_{-1,t}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ over $k[t]$ consequently embed in
$k(x)(\sqrt F)$. Thus $A_{-1,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ and
$A_{-1,t}\otimes_\mathbb Q k$ are domains. Since
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-base-open-intersection}
 (U_{-1,x})_k\cap(U_{-1,t})_k
 =\operatorname{Spec}((A_{-1,x}\otimes_\mathbb Q k)[x^{-1}])\ne\varnothing,
\end{equation}
the curve $(C_{-1})_k$ is integral as well.

To prove smoothness, put
$P_i(x,z_0,\ldots,z_g)=z_i^2-f_i(x)$ for $0\le i\le g$.
At a geometric point $a\in U_{0,x}(k)$, at most one of the
coordinates $z_i(a)$ is zero, because the polynomials $f_i$ have
disjoint sets of roots. The partial derivatives are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-affine-jacobian-entries}
 \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial z_j}=2z_i\delta_{ij},\qquad
 \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial x}=-f_i'(x).
\end{equation}
If $z_i(a)\ne0$ for all $i$, the submatrix with columns
$z_0,\ldots,z_g$ has determinant $\prod_{i=0}^g2z_i(a)\ne0$.
If $z_j(a)=0$, then $f_j'(x(a))\ne0$, and the submatrix with
columns $x$ and $z_i$ for $i\ne j$ has determinant
$\pm f_j'(x(a))\prod_{i\ne j}2z_i(a)\ne0$.
The Jacobian criterion therefore proves that $U_{0,x}$ is smooth.
At a point of $U_{0,t}\setminus U_{0,x}$, one has $t=0$ and
$v_i^2=1$ for every $i$. For
$R_i(t,v_0,\ldots,v_g)=v_i^2-h_i(t)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-infinity-jacobian-determinant}
 \det\left(\frac{\partial R_i}{\partial v_j}\right)_{0\le i,j\le g}
       =\prod_{i=0}^g2v_i\ne0\qquad\text{at }t=0.
\end{equation}
It follows that $C_0$ is smooth over $\mathbb Q$.

For $P(x,y)=y^2-F(x)$, the equalities
$\partial P/\partial y=2y$ and $\partial P/\partial x=-F'(x)$
show that the two partial derivatives cannot vanish at a point of
$U_{-1,x}$, because $F$ has no repeated root.
For $R(t,w)=w^2-\prod_{i=0}^g h_i(t)$, the equations $t=0$ and
$R(t,w)=0$ give $w=1$ or $w=-1$, and hence
$(\partial R/\partial w)(0,w)=2w\ne0$.
The Jacobian criterion proves that $C_{-1}$ is smooth over $\mathbb Q$.

By the definitions of $\rho_{-1}$ and $\rho_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-inverseimages-affine-opens}
 \begin{array}{ll}
 \rho_{-1}^{-1}(B_x)=\operatorname{Spec} A_{-1,x},&
 \rho_{-1}^{-1}(B_t)=\operatorname{Spec} A_{-1,t},\\
 \rho_0^{-1}(B_x)=\operatorname{Spec} A_{0,x},&
 \rho_0^{-1}(B_t)=\operatorname{Spec} A_{0,t}.
 \end{array}
\end{equation}
The rings $A_{-1,x}$ and $A_{-1,t}$ are free of rank two over
$\mathbb Q[x]$ and $\mathbb Q[t]$, respectively. The rings $A_{0,x}$ and
$A_{0,t}$ are free of rank $2^{g+1}$ over $\mathbb Q[x]$ and $\mathbb Q[t]$,
respectively. Thus $\rho_{-1}$ and $\rho_0$ are finite morphisms,
and $C_{-1}$ and $C_0$ are projective over $\mathbb Q$.
Finally, the field degrees give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-rho-field-degrees}
 \begin{aligned}
 \deg\rho_{-1}&=[\mathbb Q(x)(\sqrt F):\mathbb Q(x)]=2,\\
 \deg\rho_0&=[\mathbb Q(x)(\sqrt{f_0},\ldots,\sqrt{f_g}):\mathbb Q(x)]
            =2^{g+1},
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
as desired.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-galois}
The morphism $\pi_0:C_0\to C_{-1}$ is a finite Galois cover of degree
$2^g$ with group $G_0\simeq\mathbb F_2^g$. The action of
$\boldsymbol\epsilon\in G_0$ on $C_0$ is the automorphism
$\sigma_{\boldsymbol\epsilon}$ defined above.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The extension $L/\mathbb Q(x)$ is Galois of degree $2^{g+1}$, with
Galois group $\{\sigma_{\boldsymbol\epsilon}^*:
\boldsymbol\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}^{g+1}\}$.
The subgroup fixing $y=\prod_i z_i$ is $G_0$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-fixed-field-identities}
 L^{G_0}=K,\qquad
 [L:K]=2^g,\qquad \operatorname{Gal}(L/K)=G_0.
\end{equation}
The inverse images of the affine opens of $C_{-1}$ are
$\pi_0^{-1}(U_{-1,x})=U_{0,x}$ and
$\pi_0^{-1}(U_{-1,t})=U_{0,t}$.
The $A_{-1,x}$-algebra $A_{0,x}$ is generated by $z_0,\ldots,z_g$,
which satisfy the monic equations $z_i^2-f_i(x)=0$.
The $A_{-1,t}$-algebra $A_{0,t}$ is generated by $v_0,\ldots,v_g$,
which satisfy the monic equations $v_i^2-h_i(t)=0$.
Thus $A_{0,x}$ is finite over $A_{-1,x}$ and $A_{0,t}$ is finite
over $A_{-1,t}$. Consequently, $\pi_0$ is finite, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-pi-degree-group}
 \deg\pi_0=[L:K]=2^g,\qquad
 \operatorname{Aut}_{C_{-1}}(C_0)=G_0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-etale}
The morphism $\pi_0:C_0\to C_{-1}$ is \'{e}tale.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
For $0\le i\le g$, the divisor of the rational function $f_i$ on
$C_{-1}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-divisors}
 \operatorname{div}(f_i)=2W_i+2W_{i+\ell}-2P_+-2P_-.
\end{equation}
Let $P$ be a closed point of $C_{-1}$, put
$A=\mathcal O_{C_{-1},P}$, and choose a uniformizer $\varpi$ of
$A$. For $0\le i\le g$, equation
\eqref{eq:start-explicit-divisors} gives integers $n_i$ with
$v_P(f_i)=2n_i$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-local-etale-algebras}
 q_i=\varpi^{-2n_i}f_i\in A^\times,\qquad
 B_i=A[T_i]/(T_i^2-q_i).
\end{equation}
The element $T_i$ is a unit of $B_i$, since $T_i^2=q_i\in A^\times$.
Also $2\in A^\times$, so
$\partial(T_i^2-q_i)/\partial T_i=2T_i\in B_i^\times$.
It follows that $B_i$ is finite \'etale over $A$; see \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/02GH}{\textup{[Stacks, 02GH]}}.
The tensor product $B=\bigotimes_{i=0}^g B_i$ is consequently
finite \'etale over $A$.
Define an $A$-algebra homomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-local-algebra-map}
 \varphi:B\longrightarrow L,\qquad
 T_i\longmapsto\varpi^{-n_i}z_i,
\end{equation}
and put $B_0=\varphi(B)$. A finite \'etale algebra over the normal
ring $A$ is a product of normal domains. The kernel of $\varphi$
is a prime ideal whose intersection with $A$ is zero, since
$\varphi|_A$ is the inclusion $A\subset L$.
Because $B$ is integral over $A$, this kernel is a minimal prime
of $B$. Thus $B_0=B/\ker\varphi$ is one of the normal domain
factors of $B$, and is finite \'etale over $A$.
Furthermore,
$\operatorname{Frac}(B_0)=K(z_0,\ldots,z_g)=L$.
Thus $B_0$ is the integral closure of $A$ in $L$.
The curve $C_0$ is normal and finite over $C_{-1}$, with function
field $L$. Hence $C_0$ is the normalization of $C_{-1}$ in $L$,
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-local-normalization-identity}
 C_0\times_{C_{-1}}\operatorname{Spec} A=\operatorname{Spec} B_0.
\end{equation}
Since $B_0$ is finite \'etale over $A$, $\pi_0$ is \'etale at every
point above $P$. This holds for every closed point $P$ of $C_{-1}$,
and $L/K$ is separable, so $\pi_0$ is \'etale everywhere.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-genera}
The curves $C_{-1}$ and $C_0$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-genera}
 g(C_{-1})=g,\qquad g(C_0)=1+2^g(g-1).
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The degree-two morphism $\rho_{-1}:C_{-1}\to\mathbb P^1_\mathbb Q$ is
ramified precisely at $W_0,\ldots,W_{2g+1}$, with ramification
index two at each $W_a$. At $P_+$ and $P_-$, the nonvanishing
partial derivative
$\partial(w^2-\prod_i h_i(t))/\partial w=2w$
shows that $\rho_{-1}$ is unramified.
The Riemann--Hurwitz formula for $\rho_{-1}$
(\cite[Section~7.4.2]{Liu}, \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0C1B}{\textup{[Stacks, 0C1B]}}) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-rho-riemann-hurwitz}
 2g(C_{-1})-2=-4+(2g+2)=2g-2.
\end{equation}
Applying the Riemann--Hurwitz formula to the \'etale morphism
$\pi_0:C_0\to C_{-1}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-pi-riemann-hurwitz}
 2g(C_0)-2=2^g(2g(C_{-1})-2)=2^g(2g-2).
\end{equation}
Thus $g(C_{-1})=g$ and $g(C_0)=1+2^g(g-1)$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-infinity}
Write $P_1=P_+$ and $P_{-1}=P_-$. For $\delta\in\{1,-1\}$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-infinity-sign-index-set}
 E_\delta=\left\{\boldsymbol\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}^{g+1}:
                                  \prod_{i=0}^g\epsilon_i=\delta\right\}.
\end{equation}
For $\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta$, let
$Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon}\in U_{0,t}(\mathbb Q)$ be the point defined by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-infinity-points}
 t(Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon})=0,\qquad
 v_i(Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon})=\epsilon_i\quad(0\le i\le g).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-infinity-split-fibre}
 C_0\times_{C_{-1},P_\delta}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q
       =\coprod_{\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta}
                                      \{Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon}\}
       \simeq\coprod_{a=1}^{2^g}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q.
\end{equation}
The action of $G_0$ on
$\{Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon}:\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta\}$
is simply transitive.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The fibre $C_0\times_{C_{-1},P_\delta}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$ has coordinate ring
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-infinity-fibre-ring}
 \mathbb Q[v_0,\ldots,v_g]/(v_i^2-1\ (0\leq i\leq g),\,
                              \prod_i v_i-\delta).
\end{equation}
Evaluation at the tuples $\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta$ gives
an isomorphism from the ring in
\eqref{eq:start-explicit-infinity-fibre-ring} to
$\prod_{\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta}\mathbb Q$, because
$T^2-1=(T-1)(T+1)$ has distinct roots in $\mathbb Q$.
For $\boldsymbol\eta\in G_0$ and
$\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta$, the automorphism
$\sigma_{\boldsymbol\eta}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-infinity-action-formula}
 \sigma_{\boldsymbol\eta}(Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon})
       =Q_{(\eta_i\epsilon_i)_{i=0}^g}.
\end{equation}
For $\boldsymbol\epsilon,\boldsymbol\epsilon'\in E_\delta$,
there is exactly one $\boldsymbol\eta\in G_0$ with
$\eta_i\epsilon_i=\epsilon_i'$ for all $i$, namely
$\eta_i=\epsilon_i'\epsilon_i$. Thus the action of $G_0$ on
$\{Q_{\boldsymbol\epsilon}:\boldsymbol\epsilon\in E_\delta\}$
is simply transitive.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-models}
The schemes $S_{-1}$ and $S_0$ are normal integral projective flat surfaces
over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$, with pure one-dimensional fibres and respective generic
fibres $C_{-1}$ and $C_0$. The surface $S_{-1}$ is regular away from its
fibre at $2$.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Each morphism $b_{p,k}$ in
\eqref{eq:start-model-odd-blowup} or
\eqref{eq:start-model-dyadic-blowup}, and the morphism
$b_{-1}$ in \eqref{eq:start-model-resolution-morphism}, is
projective because it is a blow-up. The finite morphisms
$\nu_{-1}:X_{-1}\to P_m$ and $\Pi_0:S_0\to S_{-1}$ are
projective by \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0B3I}{\textup{[Stacks, 0B3I]}}. Composing these morphisms with
$P_m\to\mathbb P^1_\mathbb Z\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ proves that the structure
morphisms $S_{-1}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ and $S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ are
projective; see \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/01WE}{\textup{[Stacks, 01WE]}} and \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0B44}{\textup{[Stacks, 0B44]}}.
By the definition of normalization, $X_{-1}$ is normal and integral
with function field $K$, and $S_0$ is normal and integral with
function field $L$. The blow-up morphism
$b_{-1}:S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is birational, and $S_{-1}$ is
integral with function field $K$.

Fix an odd prime $p$. The support of
$\sum_a(D_a\cap P_p)+(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$ is a divisor with
normal crossings. Let $\bar z$ be a geometric closed point of
$(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$, and set
$A=\mathcal O^{\mathrm{sh}}_{P_p,\bar z}$.
Choose regular parameters $u,v$ in $A$ so that every component of
this divisor through $\bar z$ has equation $u=0$ or $v=0$.
Every unit of $A$ is a square in $A$: apply Hensel's lemma to
$T^2-a$ for $a\in A^\times$, using that $2\in A^\times$ and
that the residue field of $A$ is separably closed.
If $\bar z$ maps to the point at infinity of $\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb F_p}$,
use $\tau=x^{-1}$ and replace $F$ by
$\tau^{2g+2}F=\prod_i h_i(\tau)$; the factor
$\tau^{2g+2}$ is a square in $\operatorname{Frac}(A)^\times$.
Otherwise, the divisor of $F$ on $\operatorname{Spec} A$ is supported on
$V(u)\cup V(v)$, so there exist $b\in\operatorname{Frac}(A)^\times$ and
$\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-odd-square-class}
 F=b^2u^\alpha v^\beta.
\end{equation}
At infinity, the corresponding equality is
$\tau^{2g+2}F=b^2u^\alpha v^\beta$.
Removing $b^2$ describes the normalization over $\operatorname{Spec} A$ by
$A[z]/(z^2-u^\alpha v^\beta)$ if
$(\alpha,\beta)\ne(0,0)$, and by $A\times A$ if
$(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)$. The algebras with
$(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$ or $(0,1)$ are regular. For example,
the maximal ideal of $A[z]/(z^2-u)$ is generated by $z,v$.
For $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,1)$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-node-ring}
 B=A[z]/(z^2-uv),\qquad \mathfrak n=(u,v,z)B.
\end{equation}
The ring $B$ is normal, and $\operatorname{Spec} B\setminus\{\mathfrak n\}$
is regular: the hypersurface $\operatorname{Spec} B$ is Cohen--Macaulay,
and the only possible singular point of $\operatorname{Spec} B$ is
$V(u,v,z)$, so Serre's normality
criterion applies. Thus all singular points of $X_{-1}$ over odd
primes lie over intersections of components of the divisor on
$P_p$. This divisor has finitely many component intersections,
and $X_{-1}\to P_m$ is finite. Hence there are finitely many
singular points of $X_{-1}$ over each $p$.
For $p>R$, the sections $x=a$, $0\leq a<N$, meet
$\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb F_p}$ at distinct points, and
\eqref{eq:start-model-disjoint-prime-restriction} identifies
$P_p=\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb Z_{(p)}}$. Moreover $F$ has
valuation zero at the generic point of
$\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb F_p}$.
Thus, at each finite point, at most one of $\alpha,\beta$ in
\eqref{eq:start-model-odd-square-class} is one. At infinity,
$\prod_i h_i(0)=1$, so the algebra
$A[w]/(w^2-\prod_i h_i(t))$ is \'etale over $A$: the element
$w$ is a unit, and the derivative of its defining polynomial is
$2w\in(A[w]/(w^2-\prod_i h_i(t)))^\times$.
The regularity of the corresponding local algebras proved above
shows that $X_{-1}$ has no singular point over $p>R$. Therefore
$Z_{-1}$ is finite.

The blow-up $b_B:\operatorname{Bl}_{\mathfrak n}(\operatorname{Spec} B)
\to\operatorname{Spec} B$ has affine opens
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-node-resolution}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_u&=\operatorname{Spec} A[q]/(v-uq^2),& z&=uq,\\
 B_v&=\operatorname{Spec} A[r]/(u-vr^2),& z&=vr.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These are the opens corresponding to the generators $u$ and $v$
of $\mathfrak n$. Denote the open corresponding to the generator $z$ by
$B_z=\operatorname{Spec} B[u/z,v/z]$. The identity $(u/z)(v/z)=1$ in its
coordinate ring makes both $u/z$ and $v/z$ units and gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-resolution-open-inclusion}
 B_z\subseteq B_u\cap B_v.
\end{equation}
Let $\xi$ be a closed point of the exceptional divisor in $B_u$,
and let $b\in A$ lift the residue of $q$ at $\xi$.
The relation $v=uq^2$ shows that the maximal ideal of
$\mathcal O_{B_u,\xi}$ is generated by $u,q-b$.
This local ring has dimension two and is regular.
For a closed point $\xi\in B_v$ on the exceptional divisor,
the relation $u=vr^2$ gives the generators $v,r-b$, where
$b\in A$ lifts the residue of $r$; thus
$\mathcal O_{B_v,\xi}$ is also regular.
Writing $E_B=b_B^{-1}(\mathfrak n)$, the restriction of $b_B$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-resolution-complement}
 \operatorname{Bl}_{\mathfrak n}(\operatorname{Spec} B)\setminus E_B
     \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Spec} B\setminus\{\mathfrak n\}.
\end{equation}
The target of \eqref{eq:start-model-resolution-complement} is
regular, as proved after \eqref{eq:start-model-node-ring}.
Therefore $\operatorname{Bl}_{\mathfrak n}(\operatorname{Spec} B)$ is regular.
Applying this calculation at every point of $Z_{-1}$ proves that
$S_{-1}$ is regular over every odd prime. The generic fibre of
$S_{-1}$ is $C_{-1}$ and is regular. Over $2$, equation
\eqref{eq:start-model-resolution-at-two} identifies $S_{-1}$
with the normal scheme $X_{-1}$. Hence $S_{-1}$ is normal
everywhere and regular away from its fibre over $2$.

For $j\in\{-1,0\}$, every local ring of $S_j$ is a subring of
its characteristic-zero function field. These local rings are
torsion-free over $\mathbb Z$, and hence flat over $\mathbb Z$ by \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0AUW}{\textup{[Stacks, 0AUW]}};
see also \cite[Section~4.3.1]{Liu}.
The generic fibre of $X_{-1}$ is the normalization of
$\mathbb P^1_\mathbb Q$ in $K$, namely $C_{-1}$.
The centres of $b_{-1}$ lie in special fibres, so
$(S_{-1})_\mathbb Q=(X_{-1})_\mathbb Q=C_{-1}$.
The generic fibre of $S_0$ is the normalization of $C_{-1}$ in
$L$, namely $C_0$.
Finally apply \eqref{eq:bg-explicit-fibre-dimension} to an affine
open $\operatorname{Spec} A\subset S_j$ containing the generic point
$\mathfrak q$ of an irreducible component of $(S_j)_{\mathbb F_p}$.
Here $\operatorname{ht}(\mathfrak q)=1$ and
$\operatorname{trdeg}_\mathbb Q\operatorname{Frac}(A)=1$, so that equation gives
$\operatorname{trdeg}_{\mathbb F_p}\kappa(\mathfrak q)=1$.
Thus every irreducible component of $(S_j)_{\mathbb F_p}$ has
dimension one, as asserted; see \cite[Section~8.3.1]{Liu} and
\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/02IJ}{\textup{[Stacks, 02IJ]}}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-integral-galois}
The morphism $\Pi_0:S_0\to S_{-1}$ is finite and surjective of
degree $2^g$. The extension of function fields induced by $\Pi_0$
is the Galois extension $L/K$, with group $G_0\simeq\mathbb F_2^g$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-automorphism-group}
 \operatorname{Aut}_{S_{-1}}(S_0)=\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)=G_0.
\end{equation}
The generic fibre of $\Pi_0$ is $\pi_0:C_0\to C_{-1}$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The morphism $\Pi_0$ is finite by finiteness of normalization over
$\mathbb Z$. For every affine open $\operatorname{Spec} A\subset S_{-1}$, lying over
for the integral extension $A\subset\overline A^{\,L}$ proves
that $\operatorname{Spec}\overline A^{\,L}\to\operatorname{Spec} A$ is surjective.
Consequently $\Pi_0$ is surjective, and
$\deg(\Pi_0)=[L:K]=2^g$.
The generic fibre of $\Pi_0$ is the normalization of $C_{-1}$ in
$L$, so $(\Pi_0)_\mathbb Q=\pi_0$.

Let $\sigma\in\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)$ and
$u\in\overline A^{\,L}$.
If $u^d+a_1u^{d-1}+\cdots+a_d=0$ with $a_i\in A$, then
$\sigma$ fixes each $a_i$ and gives
$\sigma(u)^d+a_1\sigma(u)^{d-1}+\cdots+a_d=0$.
Thus $\sigma(\overline A^{\,L})=\overline A^{\,L}$.
Restriction of $\sigma$ defines an automorphism of
$\operatorname{Spec}\overline A^{\,L}$ over $\operatorname{Spec} A$.
For $f\in A\setminus\{0\}$, this automorphism restricts to
the same automorphism of
$\operatorname{Spec}(\overline A^{\,L}[f^{-1}])$ as the action of $\sigma$
on the integral closure of $A[f^{-1}]$ in $L$.
Hence these affine automorphisms define an automorphism of $S_0$
over $S_{-1}$. Conversely an automorphism of $S_0$ over $S_{-1}$
acts on $L$ and fixes $K$, and its action on $L$ determines its
action on every subring $\overline A^{\,L}\subset L$.
This proves \eqref{eq:start-model-automorphism-group}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-good-fibres}
The morphisms $S_{-1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U\to U$ and
$S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U\to U$ are smooth and projective, with
geometrically integral one-dimensional fibres. The morphism
$\Pi_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U$ is a finite \'etale $G_0$-torsor.
For every prime $p>R$, the fibre $(S_{-1})_{\mathbb F_p}$ is
obtained by gluing
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-good-fibre-base}
 \operatorname{Spec}\mathbb F_p[x,y]/(y^2-F(x))\quad\text{and}\quad
 \operatorname{Spec}\mathbb F_p[t,w]/\bigl(w^2-\prod_i h_i(t)\bigr),
\end{equation}
and the fibre $(S_0)_{\mathbb F_p}$ is obtained by gluing
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-explicit-good-fibre-cover}
 \operatorname{Spec}\mathbb F_p[x,z_0,\ldots,z_g]/(z_i^2-f_i(x))_i
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 \operatorname{Spec}\mathbb F_p[t,v_0,\ldots,v_g]/(v_i^2-h_i(t))_i.
\end{equation}
For \eqref{eq:start-explicit-good-fibre-base}, identify the open
where $x\ne0$ in the left-hand scheme with the open where $t\ne0$
in the right-hand scheme by $t=x^{-1}$ and $w=x^{-(g+1)}y$.
For \eqref{eq:start-explicit-good-fibre-cover}, identify the open
where $x\ne0$ in the left-hand scheme with the open where $t\ne0$
in the right-hand scheme by $t=x^{-1}$ and $v_i=x^{-1}z_i$.
The morphism $(\Pi_0)_{\mathbb F_p}$ is induced by
$y\mapsto\prod_i z_i$ on the affine opens with coordinate $x$
and by $w\mapsto\prod_i v_i$ on the affine opens with coordinate $t$.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Set $\Lambda=\mathbb Z[1/\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p]$, so $U=\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda$,
and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-good-rings}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_{-1,x}&=\Lambda[x,y]/(y^2-F(x)),&
 B_{-1,t}&=\Lambda[t,w]/\bigl(w^2-\prod_i h_i(t)\bigr),\\
 B_{0,x}&=\Lambda[x,z_0,\ldots,z_g]/(z_i^2-f_i(x))_i,&
 B_{0,t}&=\Lambda[t,v_0,\ldots,v_g]/(v_i^2-h_i(t))_i.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The substitutions in \eqref{eq:start-explicit-base-overlap} and
\eqref{eq:start-explicit-cover-overlap} are defined over $\Lambda$
and give isomorphisms
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-good-localizations}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_{-1,t}[t^{-1}]&\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }B_{-1,x}[x^{-1}],&
 t&\longmapsto x^{-1},&w&\longmapsto x^{-(g+1)}y,\\
 B_{0,t}[t^{-1}]&\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }B_{0,x}[x^{-1}],&
 t&\longmapsto x^{-1},&v_i&\longmapsto x^{-1}z_i.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For $j\in\{-1,0\}$, let $Y_j$ be the scheme obtained by gluing
$\operatorname{Spec} B_{j,x}$ to $\operatorname{Spec} B_{j,t}$ along
$\operatorname{Spec} B_{j,x}[x^{-1}]$ and $\operatorname{Spec} B_{j,t}[t^{-1}]$ using the
corresponding isomorphism in
\eqref{eq:start-model-good-localizations}.
The inclusions of $\Lambda[x]$ and $\Lambda[t]$ into these rings
define finite morphisms $Y_j\to\mathbb P^1_U$.
All four rings in \eqref{eq:start-model-good-rings} are free over
the corresponding polynomial ring, with the monomial bases used in
Proposition~\ref{prop:start-curves}; in particular, $Y_j\to U$
is flat.

Let $k$ be an algebraically closed residue field of a point of $U$.
The images of $0,\ldots,2g+1$ in $k$ are distinct and
$2\in k^\times$.
At a point of $\operatorname{Spec}(B_{0,x}\otimes_\Lambda k)$ with every
$z_i\ne0$, the partial derivatives
$\partial(z_i^2-f_i(x))/\partial z_i=2z_i$ give a nonzero
diagonal $(g+1)\times(g+1)$ minor of the Jacobian matrix.
If $z_j=0$, then $z_i\ne0$ for $i\ne j$ and
$f'_j(x)\ne0$; the columns indexed by $x$ and the $z_i$ with
$i\ne j$ have determinant $\pm f'_j(x)\prod_{i\ne j}2z_i\ne0$.
At a point with $t=0$ in $\operatorname{Spec}(B_{0,t}\otimes_\Lambda k)$,
one has $v_i^2=h_i(0)=1$, so
$\partial(v_i^2-h_i(t))/\partial v_i=2v_i\ne0$ for every $i$.
For $\operatorname{Spec}(B_{-1,x}\otimes_\Lambda k)$, the derivatives of
$y^2-F(x)$ are $2y$ and $-F'(x)$; they cannot both vanish
because $F$ has distinct roots in $k$.
At $t=0$ in $\operatorname{Spec}(B_{-1,t}\otimes_\Lambda k)$,
$w^2=1$, so
$\partial(w^2-\prod_i h_i(t))/\partial w=2w\ne0$.
These derivatives prove that $(Y_{-1})_k$ and $(Y_0)_k$ are smooth.
The valuation at $x=i$ of $f_i$ is one, while the valuations of
$f_j$ for $j\ne i$ are zero. Thus the classes of the $f_i$ are
independent in $k(x)^\times/k(x)^{\times2}$, and
$F\notin k(x)^{\times2}$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-good-geometric-fields}
 K_k=k(x)(\sqrt F),\qquad
 L_k=k(x)(\sqrt{f_0},\ldots,\sqrt{f_g}).
\end{equation}
The free $k[x]$-modules $B_{-1,x}\otimes_\Lambda k$ and
$B_{0,x}\otimes_\Lambda k$ embed in $K_k$ and $L_k$, respectively,
by localizing at $k[x]\setminus\{0\}$. Likewise the free
$k[t]$-modules $B_{-1,t}\otimes_\Lambda k$ and
$B_{0,t}\otimes_\Lambda k$ embed in $K_k$ and $L_k$ using
$t=x^{-1}$ and the substitutions in
\eqref{eq:start-model-good-localizations}.
In particular, all four rings are domains, and for $j=-1,0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-good-nonempty-intersection}
 \operatorname{Spec}(B_{j,x}\otimes_\Lambda k)\cap
 \operatorname{Spec}(B_{j,t}\otimes_\Lambda k)
 =\operatorname{Spec}((B_{j,x}\otimes_\Lambda k)[x^{-1}])\ne\varnothing
 \quad\text{in }(Y_j)_k.
\end{equation}
Thus $(Y_{-1})_k$ and $(Y_0)_k$ are integral.

It follows that $Y_{-1}\to U$ and $Y_0\to U$ are smooth with
geometrically integral fibres. Since $U$ is regular, $Y_{-1}$
and $Y_0$ are regular and normal. Their function fields are $K$
and $L$, respectively. The finite morphisms
$Y_{-1}\to\mathbb P^1_U$ and $Y_0\to\mathbb P^1_U$ therefore
identify $Y_{-1}$ and $Y_0$ with the normalizations of
$\mathbb P^1_U$ in $K$ and $L$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:start-model-disjoint-prime-restriction} gives
$P_m\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U=\mathbb P^1_U$, so
$X_{-1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U=Y_{-1}$.
The scheme $Y_{-1}$ is regular, hence $Z_{-1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U$
is empty and $S_{-1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U=Y_{-1}$.
Normalizing this last scheme in $L$ gives
$S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U=Y_0$.

For $0\leq j\leq g$, define the open subschemes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-etale-opens}
 \begin{aligned}
 W_{x,j}&=\operatorname{Spec} B_{-1,x}\bigl[(\prod_{i\ne j}f_i(x))^{-1}\bigr],\\
 W_{t,j}&=\operatorname{Spec} B_{-1,t}\bigl[(\prod_{i\ne j}h_i(t))^{-1}\bigr].
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
At every point of $\operatorname{Spec} B_{-1,x}$ at most one $f_i(x)$ vanishes,
so $\bigcup_jW_{x,j}=\operatorname{Spec} B_{-1,x}$.
Likewise $\bigcup_jW_{t,j}=\operatorname{Spec} B_{-1,t}$; at $t=0$ all
$h_i(0)=1$.
Write $A_{x,j}=\Gamma(W_{x,j},\mathcal O_{W_{x,j}})$.
Define the $A_{x,j}$-algebra $B_{x,j}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-etale-algebra}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_{x,j}&=A_{x,j}[z_i:i\ne j]/(z_i^2-f_i(x))_{i\ne j},\\
 \Pi_0^{-1}(W_{x,j})&=\operatorname{Spec} B_{x,j},\qquad
 z_j=y\Big/\prod_{i\ne j}z_i.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For each $i\ne j$, we have $z_i\in B_{x,j}^{\times}$ and
$\left.\partial(Z^2-f_i(x))/\partial Z\right|_{Z=z_i}
=2z_i\in B_{x,j}^{\times}$. Thus $B_{x,j}$ is finite \'etale
of rank $2^g$ over $A_{x,j}$.
Put $A_{t,j}=\Gamma(W_{t,j},\mathcal O_{W_{t,j}})$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-etale-infinity-algebra}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_{t,j}&=A_{t,j}[v_i:i\ne j]/(v_i^2-h_i(t))_{i\ne j},\\
 \Pi_0^{-1}(W_{t,j})&=\operatorname{Spec} B_{t,j},\qquad
 v_j=w\Big/\prod_{i\ne j}v_i.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For each $i\ne j$, we have $v_i\in B_{t,j}^{\times}$ and
$\left.\partial(V^2-h_i(t))/\partial V\right|_{V=v_i}
=2v_i\in B_{t,j}^{\times}$. Thus $B_{t,j}$ is finite \'etale
of rank $2^g$ over $A_{t,j}$.
Consequently $\Pi_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U$ is finite \'etale
of degree $2^g$.
Fix a geometric point $a$ of $W_{x,j}$ and two points $q,q'$
of the fibre $\Pi_0^{-1}(a)$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:start-model-torsor-signs}
 \epsilon_i=\frac{z_i(q')}{z_i(q)}\in\{1,-1\}\quad(i\ne j),
 \qquad \epsilon_j=\prod_{i\ne j}\epsilon_i.
\end{equation}
Then $\epsilon=(\epsilon_0,\ldots,\epsilon_g)\in G_0$.
The identity $z_j=y/\prod_{i\ne j}z_i$ gives
$z_j(q')=\epsilon_jz_j(q)$, so the automorphism
$\sigma_\epsilon$ defined by $\sigma_\epsilon^*(z_i)=\epsilon_i z_i$
satisfies $\sigma_\epsilon(q)=q'$.
Every element of $G_0$ with this property must have the coordinates
in \eqref{eq:start-model-torsor-signs}; hence it equals $\epsilon$.
For $a\in W_{t,j}$ and $q,q'\in\Pi_0^{-1}(a)$, use instead
$\epsilon_i=v_i(q')/v_i(q)$ for $i\ne j$ and
$\epsilon_j=\prod_{i\ne j}\epsilon_i$. The identity
$v_j=w/\prod_{i\ne j}v_i$ again gives the unique automorphism
$\sigma_\epsilon$ with $\sigma_\epsilon(q)=q'$.
Therefore
$\Pi_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U$ is a $G_0$-torsor, as asserted.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:starting-regularity}
The normalization $S_0$ of $S_{-1}$ in $L$ is regular.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{prop:start-good-fibres} proves that
$(S_0)_U=S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}U$ is regular.
We first prove regularity of
$S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(p)}$ for an odd prime
$p\in\Sigma$.
For an irreducible component $E$ of $(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$,
let $v_E$ be the normalized valuation of the discrete valuation ring
$\mathcal O_{P_p,\eta_E}$ at the generic point $\eta_E$ of $E$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-component-inertia}
 b_E=(v_E(f_i)\bmod 2)_{0\leq i\leq g}\in H.
\end{equation}
Write $D_a\subset P_p$ for the restriction to $P_p$ of the strict
transform of $\sigma_a(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z)$ on $P_m$.
For $a=i$ or $a=i+\ell$, put $b_{D_a}=e_i$, where
$e_i=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)\in H$ has $i$th entry $1$.
For $D=D_a$, let $v_D$ be the normalized valuation of
$\mathcal O_{P_p,\eta_D}$.
If $D$ is a component of $(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$ or a divisor $D_a$,
and $w$ is an extension of $v_D$ to $L$, the inertia subgroup $I_w\subset H$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-subgroup}
 I_w=\langle b_D\rangle.
\end{equation}
To verify \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-subgroup}, let
$R_D^{\mathrm{sh}}$ be a strict henselization of the discrete
valuation ring $\mathcal O_{P_p,\eta_D}$, let $\pi$ be a
uniformizer, and put $F_D=\operatorname{Frac}(R_D^{\mathrm{sh}})$.
Write $f_i=\pi^{v_D(f_i)}u_i$ with
$u_i\in(R_D^{\mathrm{sh}})^\times$.
Since $p\ne2$, every $u_i$ has a square root in
$R_D^{\mathrm{sh}}$.
Thus every field factor of $L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}F_D$ is
$F_D$ when $b_D=0$, and is $F_D(\sqrt\pi)$ when $b_D\ne0$.
In the second case, the automorphism $\sqrt\pi\mapsto-\sqrt\pi$
acts on $\sqrt{f_i}$ by $(-1)^{v_D(f_i)}$.
Consequently $I_w=\{0\}$ when $b_D=0$, and otherwise $b_D$
is the unique nonidentity element of $I_w$.

Let $z$ be a geometric closed point of $(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$, and
let $(A,\mathfrak m_A)$ be the strict henselization of
$\mathcal O_{P_p,z}$, using the geometric point to choose the residue
field. The reduced divisor formed by $(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$ and
$D_0,\ldots,D_{N-1}$ has strict normal crossings.
Choose regular parameters $u,v$ of $A$ such that every component of
this divisor through $z$ is defined by $u=0$ or $v=0$.
Choose integers $b_i,c_i\in\{0,1\}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-local-parity-vectors}
 b_i\equiv\operatorname{ord}_{(u)}(f_i)\pmod2,\qquad
 c_i\equiv\operatorname{ord}_{(v)}(f_i)\pmod2
 \quad(0\leq i\leq g).
\end{equation}
Put $b=(b_i\bmod2)_{i=0}^g\in H$ and
$c=(c_i\bmod2)_{i=0}^g\in H$.
Here $\operatorname{ord}_{(u)}$ and $\operatorname{ord}_{(v)}$ are the
normalized valuations of $A_{(u)}$ and $A_{(v)}$.
When $u=0$ defines a component $D$ of the divisor, $b=b_D$;
when $u=0$ defines no component, $b=0$.
When $v=0$ defines a component $D$, $c=b_D$; otherwise $c=0$.
Thus \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-subgroup} identifies the inertia
subgroups at $u=0$ and $v=0$ as $\langle b\rangle$ and
$\langle c\rangle$.

At a point above $x=\infty$, replace $f_i$ by
$\tau^2f_i=h_i(\tau)$ with $\tau=x^{-1}$; multiplication by
$\tau^2$ does not change the class in
$\operatorname{Frac}(A)^\times/\operatorname{Frac}(A)^{\times2}$.
Factoring $f_i$ in $\operatorname{Frac}(A)$, using that $A$ is factorial, gives
$f_i=\xi_i u^{b_i}v^{c_i}r_i^2$ with
$\xi_i\in A^\times$ and $r_i\in\operatorname{Frac}(A)^\times$.
Since $2\in A^\times$ and $A$ is strictly henselian, every
$\xi_i$ has a square root in $A$.
Let $\widetilde A_L$ be the integral closure of $A$ in
$L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(A)$, so that
$X_0\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A=\operatorname{Spec}\widetilde A_L$.
If $b,c$ are linearly independent, $\widetilde A_L$ is a product
of copies of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-independent-normalization}
 B_{u,v}=A[s,t]/(s^2-u,t^2-v).
\end{equation}
The maximal ideal of $B_{u,v}$ is $(s,t)$, and
$\dim B_{u,v}=2$, so $B_{u,v}$ is regular.
If $b\ne0$ and $c=0$, the factors of $\widetilde A_L$ are
$A[s]/(s^2-u)$, which are regular with parameters $s,v$.
The case $b=0$, $c\ne0$ follows by interchanging $u$ and $v$.
If $b=c=0$, then $\widetilde A_L$ is a product of copies of $A$.
If $b=c\ne0$, the factors are $A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$.

Suppose $z\in E\cap F$ for two distinct irreducible components
$E,F$ of $(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$. We claim that $b_E\ne b_F$.
By the construction of $P_p$, one of the components, say $F$, was
created by blowing up a smooth point of the strict transform of $E$.
Let $B\subset\{0,\ldots,N-1\}$ consist of the indices $a$ for
which the strict transform of $\sigma_a(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z)$ passed
through the centre of that blow-up. The set $B$ is nonempty.
For $a\in B$, $v_F(x-a)=v_E(x-a)+1$; for $a\notin B$,
$v_F(x-a)=v_E(x-a)$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-after-blowup}
 b_F-b_E=\sum_{a\in B}e_{\operatorname{pair}(a)},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{pair}(a)=
 \begin{cases}a,&0\leq a\leq g,\\a-\ell,&\ell\leq a<N.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Since $p\nmid\ell$, the strict transforms of
$\sigma_i(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z)$ and $\sigma_{i+\ell}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z)$
cannot pass through the same point above $p$.
Thus the map $\operatorname{pair}:B\to\{0,\ldots,g\}$ is
injective. The sum in
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-after-blowup} is a nonempty sum
of distinct basis vectors, so $b_F-b_E\ne0$.

Suppose instead that $z\in D_a\cap E$, where $E$ is a component
of $(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$ and $a=i$ or $a=i+\ell$.
If $b=c\ne0$ in \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-local-parity-vectors},
then $b=c=e_i$, so $\lambda(b)=\lambda(c)=1$.
In this case put $R=A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$.
Then $X_{-1}\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A=\operatorname{Spec} R$, and
$X_0\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A$ is a disjoint union of copies of $\operatorname{Spec} R$.
The restriction of $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ to $\operatorname{Spec} R$ is the blow-up
of the ideal $(u,v,z_1)$.
The computation in \eqref{eq:start-explicit-node-resolution} proves
that $\operatorname{Bl}_{(u,v,z_1)}(\operatorname{Spec} R)$ is regular.
The normalization in each of the identical field factors therefore
leaves this blow-up unchanged. Hence $S_0$ is regular above $z$ in
this case.

It remains at odd primes to consider $b,c$ linearly independent
with $\lambda(b)=\lambda(c)=1$.
Again put $R=A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$; now choose $z_1=st$ in
$B_{u,v}$ from \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-independent-normalization}.
The blow-up $\operatorname{Bl}_{(u,v,z_1)}(\operatorname{Spec} R)$ contains the
affine open
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-quadratic-u-open}
 U_u=\operatorname{Spec} R_u,\qquad R_u=A[q]/(v-uq^2),\qquad
 q=z_1/u,\quad z_1=uq.
\end{equation}
The integral closure of $R_u$ in the field of fractions of
$B_{u,v}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-u-open}
 B_u=A[s,q]/(u-s^2,\ v-s^2q^2),\qquad t=sq.
\end{equation}
The ring $B_u$ is finite over $R_u$, and the formulas
$s\mapsto s$, $q\mapsto t/s$ identify
$\operatorname{Frac}(B_u)=\operatorname{Frac}(B_{u,v})$.
To check normality, we prove that $B_u$ is regular: at a closed point $\xi$ of
$\operatorname{Spec} B_u$ with $s(\xi)=0$, choose $\beta\in A$ lifting $q(\xi)$.
The maximal ideal of $(B_u)_\xi$ is $(s,q-\beta)$ and
$\dim(B_u)_\xi=2$.
The affine open associated with the generator $v$ is
$U_v=\operatorname{Spec} R_v$, where
$R_v=A[q']/(u-v(q')^2)$ and $q'=z_1/v$.
Its integral closure in the field of fractions of $B_{u,v}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-v-open}
 B_v=A[t,q']/(v-t^2,\ u-t^2(q')^2),\qquad s=tq'.
\end{equation}
At a closed point $\xi'$ of $\operatorname{Spec} B_v$ with $t(\xi')=0$,
the maximal ideal of $(B_v)_{\xi'}$ is $(t,q'-\beta')$, where
$\beta'\in A$ lifts $q'(\xi')$. Thus $(B_v)_{\xi'}$ is regular.
Outside $V(s)\subset\operatorname{Spec} B_u$ and $V(t)\subset\operatorname{Spec} B_v$,
respectively, the homomorphisms
$B_{u,v}\to B_u$, $t\mapsto sq$, and
$B_{u,v}\to B_v$, $s\mapsto tq'$, give the isomorphisms
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-1}
 B_{u,v}[s^{-1}]\simeq B_u[s^{-1}],\qquad
 B_{u,v}[t^{-1}]\simeq B_v[t^{-1}].
\end{equation}
Consequently $B_u$ and $B_v$ are regular everywhere.
The affine open associated with the generator $z_1$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-quadratic-z-open}
 U_{z_1}=\operatorname{Spec} A[z_1,\alpha,\beta]/
 (u-z_1\alpha,v-z_1\beta,\alpha\beta-1),\qquad
 \alpha=u/z_1,\quad\beta=v/z_1.
\end{equation}
Since $\alpha\beta=1$, one has
$U_{z_1}=D(q)\subset U_u$ and
$U_{z_1}=D(q')\subset U_v$, with $q'=q^{-1}$.
Thus $U_u\cup U_v$ covers the blow-up, and
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-u-open}--
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-v-open} prove that $S_0$ is
regular above $z$.
If $\lambda(b)$ and $\lambda(c)$ are not both $1$, then
$X_{-1}$ is regular above $z$ and the morphism $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$
is an isomorphism there. The regularity of $B_{u,v}$ and of the
other factors already considered then proves regularity of $S_0$
above $z$.
This proves regularity over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(p)}$ for every odd
$p\in\Sigma$.

We now consider the prime $2$. Put $k=m-2$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-functions}
 A_i=-\frac{2^{m-2}}{x-i},\qquad
 \frac{f_i}{(x-i)^2}=1+4A_i\quad(0\leq i\leq g).
\end{equation}
At the generic point of $E_{j,c}\subset(P_2)_{\mathbb F_2}$,
where $j\leq k$, the valuation $v_{E_{j,c}}$ satisfies
$v_{E_{j,c}}(2)=1$ and $v_{E_{j,c}}(x-i)\leq j$.
It follows that $v_{E_{j,c}}(A_i)\geq0$.
Among the horizontal prime divisors of $P_2$, only $D_i$ can have
negative valuation on $A_i$.
The local rings of $P_2$ are regular and hence factorial
(\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0AG0}{\textup{[Stacks, 0AG0]}}). Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-2}
 A_i\in\mathcal O_{P_2,z}
 \quad\text{for every }z\in(P_2)_{\mathbb F_2}\setminus D_i.
\end{equation}
The intersection of $D_i$ with $(P_2)_{\mathbb F_2}$ lies on
$E_{k,c}$ with $c\equiv i\pmod{2^k}$.

Let $z$ be a geometric point of $(P_2)_{\mathbb F_2}$ outside
$\bigcup_a D_a$, and let $A$ be the strict henselization of
$\mathcal O_{P_2,z}$. For each $i$ define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-substitution}
 W_i=\frac{\sqrt{f_i}/(x-i)-1}{2},\qquad
 P_i(W)=W^2+W-A_i,\qquad P_i(W_i)=0.
\end{equation}
Here $A_i\in A$. Let $w_i$ be the image of the indeterminate
$W$ in $A[W]/(P_i(W))$. Then
$P_i'(w_i)=2w_i+1\in(A[W]/(P_i(W)))^\times$.
Thus $A[W]/(P_i(W))$ is finite \'etale over $A$, and strict
henselianity gives $A[W]/(P_i(W))\simeq A\times A$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-3}
 \widetilde A_L\simeq\prod_{j=1}^{2^{g+1}}A.
\end{equation}
In particular, $X_0$ is regular at every point above $z$.
This calculation applies when $z$ is an intersection of two
components of $(P_2)_{\mathbb F_2}$, since $D_a\cap E\cap F=\varnothing$ for every $a$ and
every pair of distinct fibre components $E,F$.

For $0\leq j<2^{m-1}$, let $\zeta_j$ be the point of
$E_{k,c}$ through which the divisors $D_a$ with
$a\equiv j\pmod{2^{m-1}}$ pass, where $c\equiv j\pmod{2^k}$.
These are precisely the points of
$(P_2)_{\mathbb F_2}\cap\bigcup_aD_a$.
Let $A$ be the strict henselization of $\mathcal O_{P_2,\zeta_j}$
and put $t=(x-j)/2^{m-2}$, so $\mathfrak m_A=(2,t)$.
For $i\notin\{j,j+2^{m-1}\}$, the polynomial
$P_i(W)$ from \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-substitution}
has coefficients in $A$ and defines $A\times A$ as above.
For the remaining indices put
$U=\sqrt{f_j}/2^{m-2}$ and
$V=\sqrt{f_{j+2^{m-1}}}/2^{m-2}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-two-extensions}
 U^2=t(t-4),\qquad V^2=(t-2)(t-6).
\end{equation}
Define $a,b,d$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-integral-substitutions}
 U=t+2a,\qquad V=t-2+2b,\qquad d=b-a.
\end{equation}
Substitution in \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-two-extensions}
gives the finite free $A$-algebra of rank four
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-rank-four-algebra}
 B=A[a,b]/(a^2+ta+t,\ b^2+(t-2)b+t-2).
\end{equation}
Writing $F_A=\operatorname{Frac}(A)$, one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-4}
 B\otimes_A F_A\simeq
 F_A[U,V]/(U^2-t(t-4),V^2-(t-2)(t-6)).
\end{equation}
The quotient $B/(2,t)B=\kappa(A)[a,b]/(a^2,b^2)$ has one
prime ideal. Thus the unique maximal ideal of $B$ above
$\mathfrak m_A$ is $\mathfrak n=(2,t,a,b)$.
In $B_{\mathfrak n}$ the defining relations give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-parameters}
 t(a+1)=-a^2,\qquad
 d^2+td+2\bigl((a-1)d-(a+1)\bigr)=0.
\end{equation}
The residues of $a+1$ and $(a-1)d-(a+1)$ in
$B_{\mathfrak n}/\mathfrak nB_{\mathfrak n}$ are nonzero.
Hence \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-parameters} gives
$t,2\in(a,d)B_{\mathfrak n}$, and therefore
$\mathfrak nB_{\mathfrak n}=(a,d)$.
Since $\dim B_{\mathfrak n}=2$, the ring $B_{\mathfrak n}$ is
regular and normal.
The algebra $B$ is finite over the local ring $A$ and has only the
maximal ideal $\mathfrak n$, so $B=B_{\mathfrak n}$.
Thus $B$ is the integral closure of $A$ in $B\otimes_A F_A$.
The equations for $i\notin\{j,j+2^{m-1}\}$ split over $A$, and
consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-5}
 \widetilde A_L\simeq\prod_{h=1}^{2^{g-1}}B.
\end{equation}
This proves that $X_0$ is regular above every $\zeta_j$.
Finally,
$S_{-1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}
 \simeq X_{-1}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$.
Normalization in $L$ then gives
$S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}
 \simeq X_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$. Hence
$S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$ is regular,
completing the proof.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-integral-section}
The point $Q_+\in C_0(\mathbb Q)$ extends uniquely to a section
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-section}
 s_0:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\longrightarrow S_0.
\end{equation}
The composite $s_{-1}=\Pi_0\circ s_0:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\to S_{-1}$
is the unique section extending $P_+\in C_{-1}(\mathbb Q)$.
The morphism $S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is smooth at every point
of $s_0(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z)$, and
$S_{-1}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is smooth at every point of
$s_{-1}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z)$.
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-split-section-fibre}
 S_0\times_{S_{-1},s_{-1}}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z
 \simeq\coprod_{j=1}^{2^g}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
For every prime $p$, properness gives a unique extension
$s_{0,p}:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(p)}\to S_0$ of $Q_+$ over
$\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$.
Since $S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is of finite presentation, there
are an open neighbourhood $V_p\subset\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ of $(p)$
and a morphism $s_{0,V_p}:V_p\to S_0$ whose restriction to
$\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(p)}$ is $s_{0,p}$.
For any primes $p,q$, the morphisms
$s_{0,V_p}|_{V_p\cap V_q}$ and $s_{0,V_q}|_{V_p\cap V_q}$
agree at $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$. Separatedness of
$S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-6}
 s_{0,V_p}|_{V_p\cap V_q}=s_{0,V_q}|_{V_p\cap V_q}.
\end{equation}
Therefore the morphisms $s_{0,V_p}$ define a section $s_0$ on
$\bigcup_pV_p=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$.
The uniqueness in the valuative criterion proves uniqueness of $s_0$.
Applying the same argument to $S_{-1}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ and
$P_+$ gives a unique section $s_{-1}$; since
$\pi_0(Q_+)=P_+$, uniqueness gives $\Pi_0\circ s_0=s_{-1}$.

We next check the morphisms near the sections explicitly.
Put $T=x^{-1}$ and
$H(T)=\prod_{i=0}^g(1-iT)(1-(i+\ell)T)$.
For every prime $p$ let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-7}
 R_p=\mathbb Z_{(p)}[T,H(T)^{-1}],\qquad
 \mathcal V_p=\operatorname{Spec} R_p\subset\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb Z_{(p)}}.
\end{equation}
The section $T=0$ lies in $\mathcal V_p$.
The centres of the blow-ups defining $P_m$ lie on the images of
$\sigma_0,\ldots,\sigma_{N-1}$, which are disjoint from
$\mathcal V_p$. Hence
$P_m\times_{\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb Z}}\mathcal V_p\simeq\mathcal V_p$.
For odd $p$ the elements
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-infinity-odd-functions}
 h_i(T)=T^2f_i=(1-iT)(1-(i+\ell)T)\in R_p^\times
 \quad(0\leq i\leq g)
\end{equation}
are units. Define
$B_p=R_p[v_0,\ldots,v_g]/(v_i^2-h_i(T):0\leq i\leq g)$.
For $P_i(V)=V^2-h_i(T)$ one has
$P_i'(v_i)=2v_i\in B_p^\times$.
Thus $\operatorname{Spec} B_p\to\mathcal V_p$ is finite \'etale.
Since $R_p$ is regular, $B_p$ is regular.
The fraction field of $B_p$ is $L$, so $\operatorname{Spec} B_p$ is the
restriction of $X_0$ to $\mathcal V_p$.
Likewise, $R_p[w]/(w^2-H(T))$ is finite \'etale over $R_p$
and is the integral closure of $R_p$ in $K$.
Therefore the restriction of $X_{-1}$ to $\mathcal V_p$ is
regular, and the blow-up $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is an isomorphism
on this restriction.

For $p=2$, the functions
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-infinity-dyadic-functions}
 A_i=-\frac{2^{m-2}}{x-i}
     =-\frac{2^{m-2}T}{1-iT}\in R_2
 \quad(0\leq i\leq g)
\end{equation}
are defined because $1-iT\in R_2^\times$.
Put
$B_2=R_2[W_0,\ldots,W_g]/(W_i^2+W_i-A_i:0\leq i\leq g)$.
For $P_i(W)=W^2+W-A_i$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-8}
 P_i'(W_i)=2W_i+1,\qquad
 (2W_i+1)^2=1+4A_i=\frac{1-(i+\ell)T}{1-iT}\in R_2^\times.
\end{equation}
Consequently $\operatorname{Spec} B_2\to\mathcal V_2$ is finite \'etale,
and $B_2$ is the integral closure of $R_2$ in $L$.
The integral closure of $R_2$ in the intermediate field $K$
is also finite \'etale over $R_2$.
The identification with the functions on $C_0$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-infinity-substitution}
 \frac{\sqrt{f_i}}{x-i}=1+2W_i,\qquad
 v_i=(1-iT)(1+2W_i).
\end{equation}
The restriction to $T=0$ has the explicit presentation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-9}
 B_2/TB_2=
 \mathbb Z_{(2)}[W_0,\ldots,W_g]/
 (W_i(W_i+1):0\leq i\leq g).
\end{equation}
The points with $v_i=1$ and $v_i=-1$ on the generic fibre of
$T=0$ extend through $W_i=0$ and $W_i=-1$, respectively.
Since $0\ne-1$ in $\mathbb F_2$, these sections of
$\operatorname{Spec} B_2\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$ are distinct after reduction.

For each $p$, the restrictions of $S_0$ and $S_{-1}$ to
$\mathcal V_p$ are finite \'etale over $\mathcal V_p$.
Since $\mathcal V_p\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(p)}$ is smooth,
$S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ and $S_{-1}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ are
smooth at the points above $T=0$.
The induced morphism
$S_0\times_{P_m}\mathcal V_p\to S_{-1}\times_{P_m}\mathcal V_p$
is a morphism between finite \'etale $\mathcal V_p$-schemes,
and is therefore \'etale.

For $\epsilon=(\epsilon_i)_{i=0}^g\in\{1,-1\}^{g+1}$ with
$\prod_i\epsilon_i=1$, let $Q_\epsilon\in C_0(\mathbb Q)$
be the point $T=0$, $v_i=\epsilon_i$.
Then $\pi_0^{-1}(P_+)=\{Q_\epsilon:\prod_i\epsilon_i=1\}$.
Properness extends each $Q_\epsilon$ uniquely to a section
$s_\epsilon:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\to S_0$, and
$\Pi_0\circ s_\epsilon=s_{-1}$.
For odd $p$, the sections $s_\epsilon$ have distinct tuples
$(v_i)=(\epsilon_i)$ in the fibre at $p$.
For $p=2$, the tuples
$(W_i)=((\epsilon_i-1)/2)$ are distinct in that fibre.
Thus the morphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-10}
 \coprod_{\epsilon:\prod_i\epsilon_i=1}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z
 \longrightarrow S_0\times_{S_{-1},s_{-1}}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z
\end{equation}
induced by the sections $s_\epsilon$ is an isomorphism on every
geometric fibre. Both schemes are finite \'etale of degree $2^g$
over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$, so the morphism is an isomorphism.
This proves \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-split-section-fibre}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:start-bost-charles-model}
For every $m\geq4$, the surface $S_0$ defined in
\eqref{eq:start-surface-map} is an integral regular projective flat
arithmetic surface over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$, with smooth geometrically integral
generic fibre $C_0$ and the section $s_0$.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Proposition~\ref{prop:start-models} proves that $S_0$ is integral,
projective and flat over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$, with generic fibre $C_0$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:start-curves} proves that $C_0$ is smooth
and geometrically integral.
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity} proves that $S_0$ is
regular, and Proposition~\ref{prop:start-integral-section} constructs
the section $s_0$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:starting-odd-fibres}
Let $p\in\Sigma$ be odd and put $k_p=\overline{\mathbb F}_p$.
The reduced curves $(S_{-1})_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$ and
$(S_0)_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$ are nodal.
Their irreducible components, multiplicities and local rings can be
computed as follows from $(P_p)_{k_p}$, the graph $\Gamma_p$ in
\eqref{eq:start-explicit-fibre-graph}, and the divisors $D_a$.
Recall that $X_{-1}=\operatorname{Norm}_K(P_m)$,
$X_0=\operatorname{Norm}_L(P_m)$,
$S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is the blow-up at the singular closed points
above odd primes, and $S_0=\operatorname{Norm}_L(S_{-1})$.
Items (1) and (2) describe the components of $(X_0)_{k_p}$ and
$(X_{-1})_{k_p}$, respectively. Items (3) and (4) describe the
changes under $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ and normalization in $L$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let $E$ be an irreducible component of $(P_p)_{k_p}$.
The component $E$ is obtained by base change from a component
$E^\circ\subset(P_p)_{\mathbb F_p}$.
Let $v_E$ be the normalized valuation of
$\mathcal O_{P_p,\eta_{E^\circ}}$, and choose a uniformizer $q$
of this discrete valuation ring.
Put $m_i=v_E(f_i)$,
$b_E=(m_i\bmod2)_{i=0}^g\in H$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-11}
 b_E^\perp=\{(a_i)_{i=0}^g\in H:\textstyle\sum_i a_i(b_E)_i=0\}.
\end{equation}
For $a=(a_i)\in b_E^\perp$, define $\xi_E(a)$ to be the class
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-square-classes}
 \xi_E(a)=\left[
 \overline{q^{-\sum_i a_i m_i}\prod_i f_i^{a_i}}
 \right]\in k_p(E)^\times/k_p(E)^{\times2}.
\end{equation}
In \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-square-classes}, each $a_i$
is represented by $0$ or $1$, and the bar denotes reduction in
the residue field at $\eta_{E^\circ}$ followed by extension to
$k_p(E)$.
If $E$ was created in the construction of $P_p$ with coordinate
$X=(x-c)/p^k$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-component-valuations}
 m_i=\min\{k,v_p(c-i)\}+\min\{k,v_p(c-i-\ell)\},
 \qquad v_p(0)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
For the strict transform of
$\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb F_p}\subset\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb Z}$,
take $k=0$ in \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-component-valuations}.
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-12}
 r_E=\dim_{\mathbb F_2}\langle\xi_E(a):a\in b_E^\perp\rangle,
 \qquad
 \epsilon_E=\begin{cases}0,&b_E=0,\\1,&b_E\ne0.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
There are exactly $2^{g+1-\epsilon_E-r_E}$ irreducible
components $C\subset(X_0)_{k_p}$ whose image in $(P_p)_{k_p}$
is $E$. Each component $C$ mapping onto $E$ has multiplicity
$2^{\epsilon_E}$ in
$(X_0)_{k_p}$.
Choose representatives $u_a\in k_p(E)^\times$ of $\xi_E(a)$.
The normalization $\widetilde C$ of $C$ is the smooth projective
curve with function field
$k_p(\widetilde C)=k_p(E)(\sqrt{u_a}:a\in b_E^\perp)$.
The strict transform of $C$ on $S_0$ has the same multiplicity
$2^{\epsilon_E}$.

For the genus calculation, let $\mathcal D_E$ be the set consisting
of the components $D\ne E$ of $(P_p)_{k_p}$ with
