$D\cap E\ne\varnothing$, together with the divisors $D_a$
with $D_a\cap E\ne\varnothing$.
For a component $D$ use the vector $b_D$ from
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-component-inertia}; for $D_a$ put
$b_{D_a}=e_i$ when $a=i$ or $a=i+\ell$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-rank}
 r_E=\dim_{\mathbb F_2}\langle b_E,b_D:D\in\mathcal D_E\rangle-\epsilon_E.
\end{equation}
Put $B_E=\#\{D\in\mathcal D_E:b_D\notin\langle b_E\rangle\}$.
For every component $C\subset(X_0)_{k_p}$ mapping onto $E$,
the genus of $\widetilde C$ is zero if $r_E=0$, and otherwise
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-genus}
 g(\widetilde C)=1+2^{r_E-2}(B_E-4).
\end{equation}
\item Put $F=\prod_{i=0}^g f_i$.
If $\sum_i m_i$ is odd, there is exactly one component
$C\subset(X_{-1})_{k_p}$ mapping onto $E$;
The multiplicity of $C$ is two and $k_p(C)=k_p(E)$.
If $\sum_i m_i$ is even, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-quadratic-residue}
 \overline F_E=\overline{q^{-\sum_i m_i}F}\in k_p(E)^\times.
\end{equation}
If $\overline F_E\notin k_p(E)^{\times2}$, there is exactly
one component $C\subset(X_{-1})_{k_p}$ mapping onto $E$, and
$k_p(C)=k_p(E)(\sqrt{\overline F_E})$ has degree two over
$k_p(E)$.
If $\overline F_E\in k_p(E)^{\times2}$, there are exactly two
components $C\subset(X_{-1})_{k_p}$ mapping onto $E$, and
$k_p(C)=k_p(E)$ for each component.
When $\sum_i m_i$ is even, every component mapping onto $E$
has multiplicity one.
\item Let $z\in D\cap D'$ be an intersection of two distinct
members of the union of the fibre components and
$D_0,\ldots,D_{N-1}$.
Let $A$ be the strict henselization of $\mathcal O_{P_p,z}$,
and choose regular parameters $u,v$ of $A$ so that
$D\times_{P_p}\operatorname{Spec} A=V(u)$ and
$D'\times_{P_p}\operatorname{Spec} A=V(v)$.
Put $b=b_D$ and $c=b_{D'}$. Equivalently,
$b_i=\operatorname{ord}_{(u)}(f_i)\bmod2$ and
$c_i=\operatorname{ord}_{(v)}(f_i)\bmod2$.
The inertia subgroups at $D$ and $D'$ are
$\langle b\rangle$ and $\langle c\rangle$, as in
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-subgroup}.
After interchanging $(D,u,b)$ and $(D',v,c)$ if necessary,
the integral closure of $A$ in
$L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(A)$ is a product
of copies of the following ring:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-odd-local-rings}
\begin{array}{c|c}
 \text{condition on }b,c&\text{factor of the integral closure}\\\hline
 b=c=0&A\\
 b\ne0,\ c=0&A[s]/(s^2-u)\\
 b=c\ne0&A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)\\
 b,c\text{ linearly independent}&A[s,t]/(s^2-u,t^2-v).
\end{array}
\end{equation}
The scheme $X_{-1}\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A$ has a singular point
if and only if $\lambda(b)=\lambda(c)=1$.
In this case
$X_{-1}\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A=\operatorname{Spec} A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$,
and $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ restricts to the blow-up at
$(u,v,z_1)$.
The exceptional curve is $\mathbb P^1_{k_p}$.
Its multiplicity in $(S_{-1})_{k_p}$ is one if one of $D,D'$
is a divisor $D_a$, and two if $D,D'$ are both components
of $(P_p)_{k_p}$.
\item In the notation of (3), suppose $\lambda(b)=\lambda(c)=1$.
If $b,c$ are linearly independent, the normalization of
$\operatorname{Bl}_{(u,v,z_1)}(\operatorname{Spec} A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv))$
in each field factor of
$L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(A)$ is
$\operatorname{Bl}_{(s,t)}(\operatorname{Spec} A[s,t]/(s^2-u,t^2-v))$.
There are $2^{g-1}$ exceptional components of $(S_0)_{k_p}$
mapping to $z$, each isomorphic to $\mathbb P^1_{k_p}$.
The multiplicity of each exceptional component is two if one
of $D,D'$ is a divisor $D_a$, and four if $D,D'$ are both
components of $(P_p)_{k_p}$.
If $b=c$, then exactly one of $D,D'$ is a divisor $D_a$.
In this case there are $2^g$ exceptional components of
$(S_0)_{k_p}$ mapping to $z$, each isomorphic to
$\mathbb P^1_{k_p}$ and each of multiplicity one.
\end{enumerate}
In particular, every component of $(S_{-1})_{k_p}$ has
multiplicity at most two, and every component of $(S_0)_{k_p}$
has multiplicity at most four.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Fix a component $E\subset(P_p)_{k_p}$ as in (1).
By \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-inertia-subgroup}, the inertia
subgroup of an extension of $v_E$ to $L$ is
$\langle b_E\rangle$, of order $2^{\epsilon_E}$.
After extension of the constant field to $k_p$, the residue
field extension is
$k_p(E)(\sqrt{u_a}:a\in b_E^\perp)/k_p(E)$, of degree
$2^{r_E}$.
Thus, for every component $C\subset(X_0)_{k_p}$ mapping onto $E$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-13}
 [k_p(C):k_p(E)]=2^{r_E},\qquad e(C/E)=2^{\epsilon_E}.
\end{equation}
The equality $[L:\mathbb Q(x)]=2^{g+1}$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-14}
 \#\{C\subset(X_0)_{k_p}:C\text{ irreducible},\ C\mapsto E\}
 =\frac{2^{g+1}}{2^{r_E}2^{\epsilon_E}}
 =2^{g+1-\epsilon_E-r_E}.
\end{equation}
Since $(P_p)_{k_p}$ is reduced, the multiplicity of $C$ in
$(X_0)_{k_p}$ equals $e(C/E)=2^{\epsilon_E}$.
For $K=\mathbb Q(x)(\sqrt F)$, the same valuation calculation
has ramification index two when $v_E(F)=\sum_i m_i$ is odd.
When $\sum_i m_i$ is even, the ramification index is one and
the residue algebra is
$k_p(E)[w]/(w^2-\overline F_E)$.
This algebra is a quadratic field if
$\overline F_E\notin k_p(E)^{\times2}$ and is
$k_p(E)\times k_p(E)$ otherwise. This proves (2).

To prove \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-rank}, fix a component
$C\subset(X_0)_{k_p}$ mapping onto $E$, and denote the induced
morphism of smooth projective curves by
$\varphi_C:\widetilde C\to E$.
The extension $k_p(\widetilde C)/k_p(E)$ is Galois, with
$\operatorname{Gal}(k_p(\widetilde C)/k_p(E))$ a subgroup of $H/\langle b_E\rangle$.
For $D\in\mathcal D_E$, the inertia subgroup at
$D\cap E$ is generated by the image
$b_D+\langle b_E\rangle$ in $H/\langle b_E\rangle$.
A square class in the function field of
$E\simeq\mathbb P^1_{k_p}$ is determined by the parities of
its valuations at the points of $E$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-15}
 \operatorname{Gal}(k_p(\widetilde C)/k_p(E))
 =\langle b_E,b_D:D\in\mathcal D_E\rangle/\langle b_E\rangle.
\end{equation}
Taking dimensions proves
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-rank}.
The morphism $\varphi_C$ is ramified at exactly the points
$D\cap E$ with $b_D\notin\langle b_E\rangle$.
There are $B_E$ of these points, and every nontrivial inertia
subgroup has order two.
Riemann--Hurwitz for $\varphi_C$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-odd-riemann-hurwitz}
 2g(\widetilde C)-2=2^{r_E}(-2+B_E/2).
\end{equation}
For $r_E>0$, solving
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-odd-riemann-hurwitz} gives
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-residual-genus}.
For $r_E=0$, $\varphi_C$ is an isomorphism and
$g(\widetilde C)=g(E)=0$.

The local rings in \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-odd-local-rings}
were computed in Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity}.
For the multiplicities in (3) and (4), retain the notation
$z\in D\cap D'$, $A,u,v,b,c$ of (3), ordering the divisors so
that $D$ is a component of $(P_p)_{k_p}$.
Since $(P_p)_{k_p}$ is reduced, one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-base-fibre-equation}
 p=\eta u^\alpha v^\beta,\qquad \eta\in A^\times,\qquad
 (\alpha,\beta)=
 \begin{cases}
 (1,0),&D'=D_a\text{ for some }a,\\
 (1,1),&D'\text{ is a component of }(P_p)_{k_p}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Suppose $\lambda(b)=\lambda(c)=1$ and put
$R=A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$.
On $U_u=\operatorname{Spec} R_u$, where $R_u=A[q]/(v-uq^2)$ as in
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-quadratic-u-open}, the image of $p$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-resolved-fibre-equation}
 p=\eta u^{\alpha+\beta}q^{2\beta}\quad\text{in }R_u.
\end{equation}
The exceptional divisor in $U_u$ is $V(u)$, and $q$ is a unit
at the generic point of $V(u)$. Thus the multiplicity of $V(u)$
in the fibre of $S_{-1}$ is $\alpha+\beta$, equal to one or two as asserted
in (3).
If $b,c$ are linearly independent, the integral closure of
$R_u$ in each field factor is $B_u$ from
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-u-open}.
Substituting $u=s^2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-fibre-equation}
 p=\eta s^{2(\alpha+\beta)}q^{2\beta}\quad\text{in }B_u.
\end{equation}
The exceptional divisor in $\operatorname{Spec} B_u$ is $V(s)$.
Its multiplicity is $2(\alpha+\beta)$, equal to two when
$D'=D_a$ and four when $D'$ is a fibre component.
On $U_v=\operatorname{Spec} R_v$, where $R_v=A[q']/(u-v(q')^2)$,
and on $\operatorname{Spec} B_v$ from
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-normalized-v-open}, one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-v-open-fibre-equations}
 \begin{aligned}
 p&=\eta v^{\alpha+\beta}(q')^{2\alpha} &&\text{in }R_v,\\
 p&=\eta t^{2(\alpha+\beta)}(q')^{2\alpha} &&\text{in }B_v.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The open $U_{z_1}$ of
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-quadratic-z-open} is
$D(q)\subset U_u$ and $D(q')\subset U_v$.
Thus $U_u\cup U_v$ is the entire blow-up, so
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-resolved-fibre-equation}--
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-v-open-fibre-equations} compute every
exceptional component.
There are $2^{g-1}$ field factors when $b,c$ are independent,
and each factor gives one exceptional projective line.
This proves the count and multiplicities in the first case of (4).
If $b=c$, Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity} shows that
$D'$ is a divisor $D_a$.
There are $2^g$ factors of the integral closure, all equal to
$R=A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$.
The normalization of the blow-up in each field factor is therefore
the blow-up of $\operatorname{Spec} R$ itself.
Equation~\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-resolved-fibre-equation} with
$(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$ gives multiplicity one for each of the
$2^g$ exceptional components. This proves the second case of (4).

We check the assertions about singular points of the reduced fibres.
In the regular rings $R_u,R_v,B_u,B_v$, the reduced fibre is
cut out by one parameter or the product of two distinct parameters,
as follows from
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-resolved-fibre-equation}--
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-v-open-fibre-equations}.
At points where $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is an isomorphism, the
regular factors in \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-odd-local-rings}
give the same descriptions: the reduced fibre is cut out by
$u$, $v$, $uv$, $s$, $v$, $sv$, or $st$, as appropriate.
Hence every singular point of
$(S_{-1})_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$ or $(S_0)_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$
has completed local ring $k_p[[z_1,z_2]]/(z_1z_2)$.
This proves that $(S_{-1})_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$ and
$(S_0)_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$ are nodal.

For completeness, the identifications of the local rings are
compatible with the global normalizations.
If $\operatorname{Spec} A'\subset P_m$ is affine and $f\in A'$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-16}
 (\overline{A'}^{\,L})_f=\overline{A'_f}^{\,L}.
\end{equation}
The same identity holds for an affine open of $S_{-1}$.
Thus the descriptions of $X_0$ and $S_0$ on affine opens restrict
to identical integral closures on every smaller affine open.
The restrictions of $\sqrt{f_i}\in L$ agree under these
identifications. The automorphisms
$\sqrt{f_i}\mapsto(-1)^{a_i}\sqrt{f_i}$ commute with localization,
so the identifications also respect the actions of $H$ on $X_0$
and $G_0$ on $S_0$.

We finally prove the stronger assertion needed later: every
irreducible component of
$Y_p=(S_{-1})_{k_p,\mathrm{red}}$ is smooth, and the two
branches at a node of $Y_p$ belong to distinct irreducible
components of $Y_p$.
At a point where $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is an isomorphism,
a node of $Y_p$ can occur only above an intersection $E\cap F$
of two distinct components of $(P_p)_{k_p}$, by
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-odd-local-rings}.
The two branches map onto $E$ and $F$, respectively.
An irreducible component of $Y_p$ cannot map onto two distinct
curves, so the branches belong to different components of $Y_p$.
Above $D_a\cap E$, the reduced fibre of $X_{-1}$ has only one
branch.

At a singular point of $X_{-1}$ with local ring
$R=A[z_1]/(z_1^2-uv)$, consider the blow-up
$\operatorname{Bl}_{(u,v,z_1)}(\operatorname{Spec} R)$.
In $U_u=\operatorname{Spec} R_u$, the reduced fibre is $V(u)$ if
$\beta=0$ and $V(uq)$ if $\beta=1$, by
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-resolved-fibre-equation}.
If $\beta=1$, $V(u)$ is the exceptional curve and $V(q)$ is
the strict transform of the fibre component of $\operatorname{Spec} R$
whose image on $\operatorname{Spec} A$ is $V(v)$.
The elements $u,q$ are regular parameters at $V(u,q)$, so
$V(u)$ and $V(q)$ are smooth there and have intersection
multiplicity one.
In $U_v=\operatorname{Spec} R_v$, the reduced fibre is $V(vq')$ by
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-v-open-fibre-equations}.
Here $V(v)$ is the exceptional curve and $V(q')$ is the strict
transform of the fibre component mapping onto $V(u)\subset\operatorname{Spec} A$.
The elements $v,q'$ are regular parameters at $V(v,q')$, so
$V(v)$ and $V(q')$ are smooth there and have intersection
multiplicity one.
The exceptional curve is contracted to the point $(u,v,z_1)$,
whereas either strict transform maps onto a curve of $\operatorname{Spec} R$.
Thus the exceptional curve and either strict transform are
contained in distinct irreducible components of $Y_p$.
All branches of every component of $Y_p$ are smooth by the local
rings above, and no component contains two branches at a node.
Therefore every component of $Y_p$ is smooth, and $Y_p$ is a
strict normal-crossing curve.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:starting-dyadic-fibres}
Put $k_2=\overline{\mathbb F}_2$,
$r=2^{m-2}$ and $n=2r-1$.
The fibre $(P_2)_{k_2}$ has $n$ irreducible components in total,
namely $E_{k,c}$ for $0\leq k\leq m-2$ and $0\leq c<2^k$.
In particular, there are $r$ components $E_{m-2,c}$.

The curves $(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ and $(S_0)_{k_2}$ are reduced.
The curve $(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ has $2n$ irreducible components,
each with normalization isomorphic to $\mathbb P^1_{k_2}$.
The singular points of $(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ consist of
$2(n-1)$ ordinary nodes and $2r$ points at which two smooth
branches have intersection multiplicity two.

For $k<m-2$, there are $2^{g+1}$ irreducible components of
$(S_0)_{k_2}$ mapping onto $E_{k,c}$, each with normalization
isomorphic to $\mathbb P^1_{k_2}$.
For $k=m-2$, there are $2^{g-1}$ irreducible components of
$(S_0)_{k_2}$ mapping onto $E_{m-2,c}$, each with normalization
of genus one.
Thus, writing $c_0$ for the number of irreducible components
of $(S_0)_{k_2}$ and $a_0$ for the sum of the genera of their
normalizations, one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-component-counts}
 c_0=(r-1)2^{g+1}+r2^{g-1},\qquad a_0=r2^{g-1}.
\end{equation}
The singular points of $(S_0)_{k_2}$ consist of
$2^{g+1}(n-1)$ ordinary nodes and $r2^g$ points at which two
smooth branches have intersection multiplicity two.
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
For each component $E_{k,c}$ with $k<m-2$, the construction of
$P_2$ blows up two points of $E_{k,c}$, creating the components
$E_{k+1,c}$ and $E_{k+1,c+2^k}$.
The number of components indexed by a given $k$ is therefore
$2^k$, and the total number is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-17}
 \sum_{k=0}^{m-2}2^k=2^{m-1}-1=2r-1=n.
\end{equation}
The graph $\Gamma_2$ defined in
\eqref{eq:start-explicit-fibre-graph} is a tree with $n$ vertices
and hence $n-1$ edges. Each edge of $\Gamma_2$ corresponds to
one node of $(P_2)_{k_2}$, so $(P_2)_{k_2}$ has $n-1$ nodes.

Let $\eta_{k,c}$ be the generic point of $E_{k,c}$.
For $k<m-2$, the residue of
$A_i=-2^{m-2}/(x-i)$ at $\eta_{k,c}$ is zero for every $i$.
Hence the polynomial $W^2+W-A_i$ has residue $W(W+1)$.
The residue algebra at $\eta_{k,c}$ of the normalization in $L$
is consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-18}
 \prod_{h=1}^{2^{g+1}}k_2(E_{k,c}).
\end{equation}
Thus $(X_0)_{k_2}$ has $2^{g+1}$ components mapping onto
$E_{k,c}$, each with function field $k_2(E_{k,c})\simeq k_2(X)$.

Now fix $0\leq c<2^{m-2}$ and consider $E_{m-2,c}$.
Use the coordinate $X=(x-c)/2^{m-2}$ on $E_{m-2,c}$.
For $i=c+j2^{m-2}$ with $j=0,1,2,3$, one has
$A_i=-1/(X-j)$.
The residues of $A_{c}$ and $A_{c+2^{m-1}}$ are $1/X$,
and the residues of $A_{c+2^{m-2}}$ and
$A_{c+3\cdot2^{m-2}}$ are $1/(X-1)$.
For every other index $i$, the residue of $A_i$ is zero.
Define the additive subgroup
$\wp(k_2(X))=\{h^2+h:h\in k_2(X)\}\subset k_2(X)$.
The residues of $A_0,\ldots,A_g$ span in
$k_2(X)/\wp(k_2(X))$ the two classes represented by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-residue-classes}
 \frac1X,\qquad\frac1{X-1}.
\end{equation}
Every nonzero $\mathbb F_2$-linear combination of $1/X$ and
$1/(X-1)$ has a pole of order one.
If $h\in k_2(X)$ has a pole of order $d>0$, then $h^2+h$ has
a pole of order $2d$ at the same point.
Therefore the classes of $1/X$ and $1/(X-1)$ in
$k_2(X)/\wp(k_2(X))$ are linearly independent.
For a component $C\subset(X_0)_{k_2}$ mapping onto
$E_{m-2,c}$, the normalization $\widetilde C$ consequently
has function field
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-residue-field}
 k_2(\widetilde C)=k_2(X)(w_0,w_1),\qquad
 w_0^2+w_0=1/X,\quad w_1^2+w_1=1/(X-1).
\end{equation}
The degree of $k_2(\widetilde C)/k_2(X)$ is four.
Denote the corresponding morphism by
$\varphi_C:\widetilde C\to\mathbb P^1_{k_2}$.
Over each of $X=0$ and $X=1$ there are two points of
$\widetilde C$, each of ramification index two.
To compute the different at a point above $X=0$, put $t=X$
and $w=w_0$; at a point above $X=1$, put $t=X-1$ and $w=w_1$.
The equations in \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-residue-field}
then give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-ramified-parameter}
 w^2+w=1/t,\qquad a=tw,\qquad
 a^2+ta+t=0,\qquad t=\frac{a^2}{a+1}.
\end{equation}
The element $a$ is a uniformizer at the chosen point of
$\widetilde C$, and in characteristic two
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-19}
 \frac{dt}{da}=\frac{a^2}{(a+1)^2},\qquad
 \operatorname{ord}_a\left(\frac{dt}{da}\right)=2.
\end{equation}
Thus the different exponent is two at each of the four points
of $\varphi_C^{-1}(\{0,1\})$.
The morphism $\varphi_C$ is unramified outside $\{0,1\}$.
Riemann--Hurwitz gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-riemann-hurwitz}
 2g(\widetilde C)-2=4(-2)+4\cdot2=0.
\end{equation}
Hence $g(\widetilde C)=1$.
There are $2^{g+1}/4=2^{g-1}$ components of $(X_0)_{k_2}$
mapping onto $E_{m-2,c}$.

We now compute the components of $(X_{-1})_{k_2}$.
For each $E_{k,c}$, the sum of the residues of $A_0,\ldots,A_g$
is zero: all residues vanish when $k<m-2$, and each of the two
nonzero residues in
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-residue-classes} occurs twice
when $k=m-2$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-product-substitution}
 V=\frac{y}{\prod_{i=0}^g(x-i)}
   =\prod_{i=0}^g(1+2W_i),\qquad W=\frac{V-1}{2}.
\end{equation}
The relation $V^2=\prod_i(1+4A_i)$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-product-equation}
 W^2+W=\frac{\prod_{i=0}^g(1+4A_i)-1}{4}.
\end{equation}
At the generic point of every $E_{k,c}$, the right side of
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-product-equation} has residue
$\sum_i\overline A_i=0$.
Thus the residue algebra of the normalization in $K$ is
$k_2(E_{k,c})[W]/(W(W+1))
 \simeq k_2(E_{k,c})\times k_2(E_{k,c})$.
There are exactly two components of $(X_{-1})_{k_2}$ mapping
onto each $E_{k,c}$, each with function field $k_2(E_{k,c})$.

At every $\eta_{k,c}$, the polynomial $P_i(W)=W^2+W-A_i$
has derivative $P_i'(W)=2W+1$, which is a unit in the integral
quadratic algebra over the discrete valuation ring at
$\eta_{k,c}$.
The extensions of these discrete valuation rings in $L$ and
$K$ are therefore unramified.
Every component of $(X_0)_{k_2}$ and $(X_{-1})_{k_2}$ has
multiplicity one.
The normal surfaces $X_0$ and $X_{-1}$ have Cohen--Macaulay
local rings, so their fibres have no embedded associated points.
The multiplicities equal to one therefore imply that
$(X_0)_{k_2}$ and $(X_{-1})_{k_2}$ are reduced.
The morphism $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is an isomorphism over
$\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$, and normalization in $L$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-20}
 S_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}
 \simeq X_0\times_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}.
\end{equation}
Consequently $(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ and $(S_0)_{k_2}$ are reduced,
and the component counts give
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-component-counts}.

Let $z$ be a node of $(P_2)_{k_2}$ and let $A$ be the strict
henselization of the local ring of $P_2$ at $z$.
The calculation in Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-21}
 X_{-1}\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A\simeq\coprod_{h=1}^2\operatorname{Spec} A,
 \qquad
 X_0\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A\simeq
 \coprod_{h=1}^{2^{g+1}}\operatorname{Spec} A.
\end{equation}
Thus there are exactly two nodes of $(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ and
$2^{g+1}$ nodes of $(S_0)_{k_2}$ mapping to $z$.
Since $(P_2)_{k_2}$ has $n-1$ nodes, this gives
$2(n-1)$ nodes on $(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ and
$2^{g+1}(n-1)$ nodes on $(S_0)_{k_2}$.

It remains to consider the points where $D_a$ meets
$E_{m-2,c}$.
Fix $0\leq j<2^{m-1}$, put $c\equiv j\pmod{2^{m-2}}$
with $0\leq c<2^{m-2}$, and let $\zeta_j\in E_{m-2,c}$ be the
point through which the divisors $D_a$ with
$a\equiv j\pmod{2^{m-1}}$ pass.
Let $A$ be the strict henselization of $\mathcal O_{P_2,\zeta_j}$
and put $t=(x-j)/2^{m-2}$, so $\mathfrak m_A=(2,t)$.
Use the elements $U,V$ of
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-two-extensions}, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-quadratic-substitution}
 h=t^2-6t+4,\qquad Z=\frac{UV-h}{2}.
\end{equation}
The identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-product-identity}
 t(t-2)(t-4)(t-6)=h^2-16
\end{equation}
gives $Z^2+hZ+4=0$.
The integral closure of $A$ in
$K\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(A)$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-quadratic-ring}
 R_{-1}=A[Z]/(Z^2+hZ+4).
\end{equation}
Indeed, the map $Z\mapsto(UV-h)/2$ identifies
$R_{-1}\otimes_A\operatorname{Frac}(A)$ with
$K\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(A)$.
To prove that $R_{-1}$ is normal, we verify Serre's criterion.
The ring $R_{-1}$ is a hypersurface in the regular ring $A[Z]$,
so $R_{-1}$ is Cohen--Macaulay.
After inverting $2$, the substitution $T_1=2Z+h$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-horizontal-quadratic-ring}
 R_{-1}[1/2]\simeq
 A[1/2][T_1]/(T_1^2-t(t-2)(t-4)(t-6)).
\end{equation}
The polynomial $t(t-2)(t-4)(t-6)$ has distinct roots in
characteristic zero. The equation in
\eqref{eq:fibres-mechanical-horizontal-quadratic-ring}
therefore defines a regular curve after the \'etale base changes
used to form $A$.
Hence every localization of $R_{-1}$ at a horizontal
height-one prime is regular.
For $Q(Z)=Z^2+hZ+4$, the derivative $Q'(Z)=2Z+h$ has
residue $t^2$ at each generic point of the fibre at $2$.
Since $t^2\ne0$ in $k_2(t)$, $Q'(Z)$ is a unit at every
height-one prime of $R_{-1}$ containing $2$.
For every height-one prime $\mathfrak p\subset R_{-1}$
containing $2$, the ring $(R_{-1})_{\mathfrak p}$ is therefore \'etale over a
discrete valuation ring and are regular.
Serre's criterion proves normality of $R_{-1}$.
Since $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is an isomorphism over
$\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$, one has
$S_{-1}\times_{P_m}\operatorname{Spec} A=\operatorname{Spec} R_{-1}$.

Reduction of \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-quadratic-ring}
modulo $2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-quadratic-fibre}
 R_{-1}/2R_{-1}=(A/2A)[Z]/(Z(Z+t^2)).
\end{equation}
There is exactly one point of $\operatorname{Spec}(R_{-1}/2R_{-1})$
above $\zeta_j$, namely the point with maximal ideal $(t,Z)$.
The completion of $(R_{-1}/2R_{-1})_{(t,Z)}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-22}
 k_2[[t,Z]]/(Z(Z+t^2)).
\end{equation}
The quotient by either minimal prime $(Z)$ or $(Z+t^2)$ is
$k_2[[t]]$, so the branches defined by $(Z)$ and $(Z+t^2)$
are smooth.
The intersection multiplicity of the branches $(Z)$ and
$(Z+t^2)$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-23}
 \dim_{k_2}k_2[[t,Z]]/(Z,Z+t^2)
 =\dim_{k_2}k_2[[t]]/(t^2)=2.
\end{equation}
For each $c$, the points $\zeta_j$ on $E_{m-2,c}$ are exactly
$X=0$ and $X=1$.
There are $r$ choices of $c$, so this gives $2r$ points of
$(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ with two smooth branches of intersection
multiplicity two.

For $S_0$, use the regular ring $B=B_{\mathfrak n}$ from
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-rank-four-algebra}.
Reducing \eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-parameters} modulo
$2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-rank-four-fibre}
 d(d+t)=0,\qquad t=\frac{a^2}{a+1}\quad\text{in }B/2B.
\end{equation}
Thus the completed local ring of $B/2B$ at $(a,d)$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-24}
 k_2[[a,d]]/
 \left(d\left(d+\frac{a^2}{a+1}\right)\right).
\end{equation}
The quotient by either minimal prime $(d)$ or
$(d+a^2/(a+1))$ is $k_2[[a]]$.
The branches defined by $(d)$ and $(d+a^2/(a+1))$ are smooth,
and the intersection multiplicity of these branches is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-25}
 \dim_{k_2}k_2[[a,d]]/(d,d+a^2/(a+1))
 =\dim_{k_2}k_2[[a]]/(a^2)=2.
\end{equation}
Substituting $U=t+2a$, $V=t-2+2b$ into
\eqref{eq:fibres-explicit-dyadic-quadratic-substitution} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-26}
 Z=2t+t(a+b)-2a+2ab-2\equiv td\pmod{2B}.
\end{equation}
Hence the homomorphism on the reduced fibres induced by $\Pi_0$
is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fibres-mechanical-step-27}
 R_{-1}/2R_{-1}\longrightarrow B/2B,
 \qquad t\longmapsto\frac{a^2}{a+1},\quad Z\longmapsto td.
\end{equation}
The branch $d=0$ maps to $Z=0$, and the branch $d=t$ maps
to $Z=t^2$; on either branch the map of parameters is
$t\mapsto a^2/(a+1)$.

By Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity}, the integral
closure of $A$ in
$L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(A)$ is a product
of $2^{g-1}$ copies of $B$.
Thus there are $2^{g-1}$ points of $(S_0)_{k_2}$ mapping to
$\zeta_j$, each with completed local ring
$k_2[[a,d]]/(d(d+a^2/(a+1)))$.
There are $2r$ choices of $\zeta_j$, giving
$2r\cdot2^{g-1}=r2^g$ points of $(S_0)_{k_2}$ where two
smooth branches have intersection multiplicity two.
Finally, let $z\in(P_2)_{k_2}$ be a point which is neither
a node nor one of the $\zeta_j$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity} identifies the
strict henselian local rings of $S_{-1}$ and $S_0$ above $z$
with the strict henselian local ring of $P_2$ at $z$.
Since $(P_2)_{k_2}$ is smooth at $z$, the curves
$(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ and $(S_0)_{k_2}$ are smooth at every point
mapping to $z$.
This proves that the nodes and the points above the $\zeta_j$
listed in the proposition exhaust the singular points of
$(S_{-1})_{k_2}$ and $(S_0)_{k_2}$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:realizable-subspace}
Let $\widetilde S\to S_0\to S_{-1}$ be an anchored diagram as in
\eqref{eq:realization-given-tower}, with
$G=\operatorname{Gal}(\widetilde S/S_{-1})$. Then
$\mathscr E(\widetilde S,S_{-1})$ is an $\mathbb F_2$-linear subspace of
$H^2(G,\mathbb F_2)$.
<!-- end proposition-15 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Equation~\eqref{eq:real-explicit-zero-realization} gives
$0\in\mathscr E(\widetilde S,S_{-1})$. Let
$\theta_1,\theta_2\in\mathscr E(\widetilde S,S_{-1})$.
If $\theta_1=0$ or $\theta_2=0$, then
$\theta_1+\theta_2\in\{\theta_1,\theta_2\}$ is realizable.
We may therefore assume $\theta_1\ne0$ and $\theta_2\ne0$.
For $i\in\{1,2\}$ choose a realizing cover
$r_i:T_i\to\widetilde S$, identify $\operatorname{Gal}(T_i/S_{-1})$ with
$E_i=E_{\theta_i}$, and write
$j_i:\mathbb F_2\to E_i$ and $\rho_i:E_i\to G$ for the homomorphisms in
\eqref{eq:realization-central-extension}.

Define the fibre product $W$ and the morphism $\pi$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-cover-fibre-product}
 W=T_1\times_{\widetilde S}T_2,\qquad
 \pi=r_1\circ\operatorname{pr}_{T_1}
    =r_2\circ\operatorname{pr}_{T_2}:W\to\widetilde S.
\end{equation}
Since $r_1$ and $r_2$ are finite \'etale of degree two, $\pi$ is
finite \'etale of degree four. The group
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-group-fibre-product}
 F=E_1\times_G E_2
   =\{(e_1,e_2)\in E_1\times E_2:
                       \rho_1(e_1)=\rho_2(e_2)\}
\end{equation}
acts on $W$ by $(e_1,e_2)(t_1,t_2)=(e_1t_1,e_2t_2)$.
Indeed, if $r_1(t_1)=r_2(t_2)$ and $(e_1,e_2)\in F$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-action-fibre-product}
 r_1(e_1t_1)=\rho_1(e_1)r_1(t_1)
           =\rho_2(e_2)r_2(t_2)=r_2(e_2t_2).
\end{equation}
The subgroup
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-diagonal-subgroup}
 \Delta=\{(j_1(a),j_2(a)):a\in\mathbb F_2\}\subseteq F
\end{equation}
is central. The nonidentity element of $\Delta$ has no fixed
geometric point of $W$, because $j_i(1)$ has no fixed geometric
point of the \'etale double cover $r_i:T_i\to\widetilde S$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-quotient-cover}
 Z=W/\Delta,\qquad p_Z:Z\to\widetilde S,
\end{equation}
by the relative spectrum
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-quotient-algebra}
 Z=\operatorname{Spec}_{\widetilde S}
       \bigl((\pi_*\mathcal O_W)^\Delta\bigr).
\end{equation}
We verify that $p_Z$ is finite \'etale of degree two.

Choose an \'etale covering $\{V_\alpha\to\widetilde S\}_\alpha$
and isomorphisms
$T_i\times_{\widetilde S}V_\alpha\simeq V_\alpha\times\mathbb F_2$
for $i=1,2$, compatible with the actions of $j_i(\mathbb F_2)$.
Here $V_\alpha\times\mathbb F_2$ denotes the disjoint union of two copies
of $V_\alpha$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-quotient-trivializations}
 W\times_{\widetilde S}V_\alpha
       \simeq V_\alpha\times(\mathbb F_2\times\mathbb F_2),\qquad
 (j_1(1),j_2(1)):(a,b)\longmapsto(a+1,b+1).
\end{equation}
The two orbits of the action in
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-quotient-trivializations} are indexed by
$a+b\in\mathbb F_2$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-local-invariant-algebra}
 ((\pi_*\mathcal O_W)^\Delta)|_{V_\alpha}
       \simeq\mathcal O_{V_\alpha}\times\mathcal O_{V_\alpha},
 \qquad Z\times_{\widetilde S}V_\alpha\simeq V_\alpha\times\mathbb F_2.
\end{equation}
For completeness, on
$V_{\alpha\beta}=V_\alpha\times_{\widetilde S}V_\beta$, the
transition maps for $T_i$ have the form
$a\mapsto a+\epsilon_{i,\alpha\beta}$, where
$\epsilon_{i,\alpha\beta}:V_{\alpha\beta}\to\mathbb F_2$ is locally constant.
The induced transition map for $a+b$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-quotient-transition}
 u\longmapsto u+\epsilon_{1,\alpha\beta}
                  +\epsilon_{2,\alpha\beta}.
\end{equation}
On $V_\alpha\times_{\widetilde S}V_\beta
\times_{\widetilde S}V_\gamma$, the transition maps in
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-quotient-transition} satisfy the cocycle
identity because the transition maps of $T_1$ and $T_2$ do.
Thus \eqref{eq:real-explicit-local-invariant-algebra} identifies
$p_Z$ \'etale locally with the projection
$V_\alpha\times\mathbb F_2\to V_\alpha$, proving that $p_Z$ is finite
\'etale of degree two (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/03RV}{\textup{[Stacks, 03RV]}}). The argument applies in every
residue characteristic.

Since $\Delta$ is central in $F$, the action of $F$ on $W$ induces
an action on $Z$ of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-quotient-group}
 E=F/\Delta.
\end{equation}
The homomorphism
$\rho:E\to G$, defined by
$\rho([(e_1,e_2)])=\rho_1(e_1)=\rho_2(e_2)$, has central kernel
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-baer-kernel}
 \ker\rho=(j_1(\mathbb F_2)\times j_2(\mathbb F_2))/\Delta\simeq\mathbb F_2,
 \qquad [(j_1(a),j_2(b))]\longmapsto a+b.
\end{equation}
To compute the class of the extension, choose cocycles
$c_i=c_{\theta_i}$ and use
\eqref{eq:realization-cocycle-product} to identify $F$ with
$G\times\mathbb F_2\times\mathbb F_2$. The multiplication in $F$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-fibre-product-multiplication}
 (g,a,b)(h,a',b')
   =(gh,a+a'+c_1(g,h),b+b'+c_2(g,h)).
\end{equation}
The map $(g,a,b)\mapsto(g,a+b)$ identifies $E$ with
$G\times\mathbb F_2$ equipped with multiplication
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-baer-multiplication}
 (g,u)(h,v)=(gh,u+v+c_1(g,h)+c_2(g,h)).
\end{equation}
It follows that the extension
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-baer-extension}
 1\longrightarrow\mathbb F_2\longrightarrow E\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }G
   \longrightarrow1
\end{equation}
represents the Baer sum $\theta_1+\theta_2$.

Let $\bar\eta$ be a geometric generic point of $S_{-1}$.
The action of $E_i$ on $(T_i)_{\bar\eta}$ is simply transitive.
By the definition of $F$ in
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-group-fibre-product}, the action of $F$ on
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-generic-fibre-pairs}
 W_{\bar\eta}
   =\{(t_1,t_2)\in(T_1)_{\bar\eta}\times(T_2)_{\bar\eta}:
                                      r_1(t_1)=r_2(t_2)\}
\end{equation}
is simply transitive. Therefore the action of $E=F/\Delta$ on
$Z_{\bar\eta}=W_{\bar\eta}/\Delta$ is simply transitive.

If $\theta_1+\theta_2=0$, equation
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-zero-realization} already realizes the sum.
Suppose $\theta_1+\theta_2\ne0$. We claim that $Z$ is connected.
If $Z$ were disconnected, then, because $p_Z$ is finite \'etale of
degree two and $\widetilde S$ is connected, we could write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-disconnected-quotient}
 Z=Z_1\amalg Z_2,\qquad
 p_Z|_{Z_i}:Z_i\xrightarrow{\sim}\widetilde S\quad(i=1,2).
\end{equation}
Let $\tau$ be the nonidentity element of $\ker\rho$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:real-explicit-local-invariant-algebra} shows that
$\tau$ acts on each fibre of $p_Z$ by interchanging the two points.
Hence $\tau(Z_1)=Z_2$ and $\tau(Z_2)=Z_1$.
Put $E_{Z_1}=\{e\in E:e(Z_1)=Z_1\}$.
Given $g\in G$, choose $e\in E$ with $\rho(e)=g$. Either
$e(Z_1)=Z_1$ or $(\tau e)(Z_1)=Z_1$, so
$\rho(E_{Z_1})=G$. Moreover,
$E_{Z_1}\cap\ker\rho=\{1\}$, since $\tau(Z_1)=Z_2$.
Thus $\rho|_{E_{Z_1}}:E_{Z_1}\to G$ is an isomorphism, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-forbidden-splitting}
 G\xrightarrow{(\rho|_{E_{Z_1}})^{-1}}E_{Z_1}\hookrightarrow E
\end{equation}
splits \eqref{eq:real-explicit-baer-extension}. This contradicts
$\theta_1+\theta_2\ne0$ and proves that $Z$ is connected.

The scheme $\widetilde S$ is regular because $h:\widetilde S\to S_0$
is \'etale and $S_0$ is regular. The scheme $Z$ is regular because
$p_Z$ is \'etale. Since $Z$ is connected and regular, $Z$ is integral.
The simply transitive action on $Z_{\bar\eta}$ identifies
$\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(Z)/\mathbb Q(S_{-1}))$ with $E$.
Furthermore, $Z\to S_{-1}$ is finite, and $Z$ is normal; hence $Z$
is the normalization of $S_{-1}$ in $\mathbb Q(Z)$.
The cover $p_Z:Z\to\widetilde S$ therefore realizes
$\theta_1+\theta_2$. We have proved that
$\mathscr E(\widetilde S,S_{-1})$ contains zero and is closed under
addition. Since $\mathbb F_2=\mathbb F_2$, it is an $\mathbb F_2$-linear subspace.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-16: body_md -->
\label{prop:realizing-surface-properties}
Let $S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ be an integral regular projective flat arithmetic
surface with smooth geometrically integral generic fibre and a section
$s_0$. Let $f:T\to S_0$ be a connected finite \'etale cover of degree $d$.
Then $T$ is an integral regular projective flat arithmetic surface with
smooth geometrically integral generic fibre. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-section-pullback}
 T\times_{S_0,s_0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z
    \simeq\coprod_{i=1}^{d}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}
In particular $T\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ has a section.
<!-- end proposition-16 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Since $S_0$ is regular and $f$ is \'etale, $T$ is regular.
The irreducible components of a regular noetherian scheme are disjoint
and open. The connectedness of $T$ therefore implies that $T$ is
integral. The finite morphism $f:T\to S_0$ is projective; composing
$f$ with the projective morphism $S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ proves that
$T\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is projective. Since $f$ and $S_0\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ are flat,
the composite $T\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is flat. For every prime $p$, the map
$f_{\mathbb F_p}:T_{\mathbb F_p}\to(S_0)_{\mathbb F_p}$ is finite
\'etale and surjective, so every irreducible component of
$T_{\mathbb F_p}$ has dimension one. Also,
$f_{\mathbb Q}:T_{\mathbb Q}\to(S_0)_{\mathbb Q}$ is \'etale and $(S_0)_{\mathbb Q}$ is smooth.
It follows that $T_{\mathbb Q}$ is an integral smooth projective curve.

Put $P=T\times_{S_0,s_0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ and denote the projections by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-section-projections}
 \operatorname{pr}_T:P\to T,\qquad
 \operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb Z}:P\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z.
\end{equation}
The morphism $\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb Z}$ is finite \'etale of degree $d$.
Write $P=\coprod_{i\in I}P_i$ for the decomposition into connected
components. Each $P_i$ is isomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{K_i}$
for a number field $K_i$ unramified at every finite prime.
Consequently $|\operatorname{disc}(K_i)|=1$, and Minkowski's
discriminant bound gives $K_i=\mathbb Q$
\cite[Theorem~4.9 and its proof]{MilneANT}.
Thus $\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb Z}|_{P_i}:P_i\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is an isomorphism
for every $i\in I$, and $|I|=d$. This proves
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-section-pullback}.
For each $i\in I$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-lifted-section}
 s_i=\operatorname{pr}_T|_{P_i}\circ
       (\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb Z}|_{P_i})^{-1}:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\to T.
\end{equation}
If $\tau:T\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$ is the structure morphism, then
$\tau\circ s_i=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z}$ and $f\circ s_i=s_0$.
In particular, $(s_i)_{\mathbb Q}$ is a point of $T_{\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q)$.

Let $\mathcal C$ be the set of connected components of
$T_{\overline\mathbb Q}$. Since $T_{\mathbb Q}$ is connected,
$\operatorname{Gal}(\overline\mathbb Q/\mathbb Q)$ acts transitively on $\mathcal C$.
Choose $i\in I$, and let $C_i\in\mathcal C$ be the component
containing the geometric point defined by $(s_i)_{\mathbb Q}$.
The rationality of $(s_i)_{\mathbb Q}$ gives
$\sigma(C_i)=C_i$ for every $\sigma\in\operatorname{Gal}(\overline\mathbb Q/\mathbb Q)$.
Transitivity now implies $\mathcal C=\{C_i\}$.
Since $T_{\overline\mathbb Q}$ is smooth, connectedness implies integrality.
Thus $T_{\mathbb Q}$ is geometrically integral.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-17: body_md -->
\label{prop:starting-anchor}
Let $S_0\to S_{-1}$ be the morphism of
Section~\ref{sec:starting-surface}, with $g=2^m-1$ and $m\ge4$.
If $\widetilde S\to S_0$ is a connected finite \'etale cover and
$\widetilde S\to S_{-1}$ is Galois, then
$\widetilde S\to S_0\to S_{-1}$ is an anchored diagram.
<!-- end proposition-17 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Propositions~\ref{prop:start-models},
\ref{prop:start-integral-galois}, \ref{prop:starting-regularity} and
\ref{prop:start-integral-section} prove (i), construct the section
$s_0:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z\to S_0$, and show that
$q:S_0\to S_{-1}$ is Galois with group $A=G_0\simeq\mathbb F_2^g$ and
$q_{\mathbb Q}$ is \'etale. The hypotheses on $\widetilde S\to S_0$ and
$\widetilde S\to S_{-1}$ give the remaining assertions in (ii).
Take $\Sigma=\{p:p\text{ prime},\ p\le2g+1\}$ and let $U$ be as in
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-good-base}.
Proposition~\ref{prop:start-good-fibres} shows that
$(S_{-1})_U\to U$ is smooth with geometrically connected fibres and
$q_U:(S_0)_U\to(S_{-1})_U$ is an \'etale $A$-torsor.

For $0\le a\le2g+1$, put $W_a=(a,0)\in C_{-1}(\mathbb Q)$.
The points $W_a$ are the ramification points of
$\rho_{-1}:C_{-1}\to\mathbb P^1_{\mathbb Q}$.
We will prove that the classes
$\delta_a=[W_a-W_{2g+1}]$, for $0\le a\le2g$, generate
$J(C_{-1})(\overline\mathbb Q)[2]$.
First, $2\delta_a=0$, because
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-branch-divisor}
 \operatorname{div}\!\left(\frac{x-a}{x-(2g+1)}\right)
     =2W_a-2W_{2g+1}.
\end{equation}
To determine the relations among the $\delta_a$, let
$I\subseteq\{0,\ldots,2g\}$ and suppose
$\sum_{a\in I}\delta_a=0$.
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-subset-product}
 R_I(x)=\prod_{a\in I}\frac{x-a}{x-(2g+1)}.
\end{equation}
The assumed relation gives $h\in\overline\mathbb Q(C_{-1})^\times$ with
$\operatorname{div}(h)=\sum_{a\in I}(W_a-W_{2g+1})$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:real-explicit-branch-divisor} implies
$\operatorname{div}(R_I/h^2)=0$, so
$R_I/h^2\in\overline\mathbb Q^\times$ and
$R_I\in\overline\mathbb Q(C_{-1})^{\times2}$.
Recall that
$\overline\mathbb Q(C_{-1})=\overline\mathbb Q(x)(\sqrt F)$, where
$F=\prod_{a=0}^{2g+1}(x-a)$.
If $(u+v\sqrt F)^2\in\overline\mathbb Q(x)$, with
$u,v\in\overline\mathbb Q(x)$, the coefficient of $\sqrt F$ is $2uv$;
hence $u=0$ or $v=0$. It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-square-intersection}
 \overline\mathbb Q(x)^\times\cap\overline\mathbb Q(C_{-1})^{\times2}
   =\overline\mathbb Q(x)^{\times2}\ \cup\ F\overline\mathbb Q(x)^{\times2}.
\end{equation}
For $0\le a\le2g$, the order of vanishing of $R_I$ at $x=a$ is
$1$ if $a\in I$ and $0$ if $a\notin I$.
If $R_I\in\overline\mathbb Q(x)^{\times2}$, then
$\operatorname{ord}_{x=a}(R_I)\equiv0\pmod2$ for every
$0\le a\le2g$, so $I=\varnothing$. If
$R_I\in F\overline\mathbb Q(x)^{\times2}$, then
$\operatorname{ord}_{x=a}(R_I)\equiv1\pmod2$ for every
$0\le a\le2g$, so $I=\{0,\ldots,2g\}$.
Finally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-full-relation-divisor}
 \operatorname{div}\!\left(\frac{y}{(x-(2g+1))^{g+1}}\right)
   =\sum_{a=0}^{2g}(W_a-W_{2g+1})
\end{equation}
gives $\sum_{a=0}^{2g}\delta_a=0$.
Thus the $2g+1$ classes $\delta_a$ have precisely one nonzero
$\mathbb F_2$-linear relation, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-two-torsion-span}
 \dim_{\mathbb F_2}\langle\delta_0,\ldots,\delta_{2g}\rangle=2g
    =\dim_{\mathbb F_2}J(C_{-1})(\overline\mathbb Q)[2].
\end{equation}
Since every $\delta_a$ is rational over $\mathbb Q$, equation
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-two-torsion-span} proves
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-rational-two-torsion} and completes (iii).

Fix an odd prime $p\in\Sigma$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-odd-fibres} shows that
$Y_p=(S_{-1})_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p,\mathrm{red}}$ has strict normal
crossings. Let
$n_p=|V_p|$ be the number of irreducible components of
$(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$, where
$\Gamma_p=(V_p,\mathcal E_p,\partial_p)$ is defined in
\eqref{eq:start-explicit-fibre-graph}.
In the construction of $P_m$, a blow-up at stage $k\ge1$ over $p$
has centre indexed by a residue class modulo $p^k$ containing at
least two integers from $\{0,\ldots,N-1\}$.
At stage $k$ there are at most $p^k$ centres, and a centre can occur
only if $p^k\le N-1$. Each blow-up adds one irreducible component to
the geometric fibre. Therefore, on putting
$M_p=\lfloor\log_p(N-1)\rfloor$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-base-component-bound}
 n_p\le1+\sum_{k=1}^{M_p}p^k
       \le1+\frac{p}{p-1}(N-1)\le1+\frac32N.
\end{equation}
The divisors $D_a$, for $0\le a<N$, meet
$(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$ at $N$ distinct smooth points.
Also, $\Gamma_p$ is a tree, so
$|\mathcal E_p|=n_p-1$; equivalently,
$(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$ has $n_p-1$ nodes.
Define the finite set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-base-intersections}
 \mathcal I_p=
 \operatorname{Sing}((P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p})
 \cup\bigcup_{a=0}^{N-1}
       (D_a\cap(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}),
 \qquad |\mathcal I_p|=N+n_p-1.
\end{equation}
The branch divisor of $X_{-1}\to P_m$ is supported on the union of
the $D_a$ and the components of the special fibres of $P_m$.
The local equations of Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-odd-fibres}
show that every singular point of $X_{-1}$ over $p$ maps to a point
of $\mathcal I_p$, and that at most one singular point maps to each
element of $\mathcal I_p$.
The curve $(X_{-1})_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p,\mathrm{red}}$ has at
most $2n_p$ irreducible components.
In the construction of $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$, the blow-up at each
singular point of $X_{-1}$ over $p$ introduces one rational component.
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-odd-fibres} also proves that $Y_p$ has
at most four nodes above each node of
$(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$, and at most two nodes above each
point of $D_a\cap(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$.
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-odd-component-node-bound}
 c_p\le2n_p+(N+n_p-1)=3n_p+N-1\le7N,\qquad
 s_p\le4(n_p-1)+2N\le8N.
\end{equation}

For each irreducible component $E$ of
$(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$, let $\mathcal C_E$ be the set of
components of $(X_{-1})_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p,\mathrm{red}}$
whose image in $P_p$ is $E$.
For $Z\in\mathcal C_E$, let $\widetilde Z$ be the normalization
of $Z$ and let $\phi_Z:\widetilde Z\to E$ be the induced morphism.
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-odd-fibres} gives
$\deg\phi_Z\in\{1,2\}$.
For a degree-two morphism $\phi_Z$, let $b_Z$ be the number of
branch points on $E$.
A point of $D_a\cap(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$ contributes at
most one branch point, and a node of
$(P_p)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$ contributes at most one branch
point on each of the two components containing that node. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-branch-count}
 \sum_{E\in V_p}\sum_{\substack{Z\in\mathcal C_E\\\deg\phi_Z=2}}
 b_Z\le N+2(n_p-1)\le4N.
\end{equation}
For $\deg\phi_Z=2$, Riemann--Hurwitz gives
$2g(\widetilde Z)-2=-4+b_Z$, because
$E\simeq\mathbb P^1_{\overline{\mathbb F}_p}$ and $p\ne2$.
In particular, $g(\widetilde Z)\le b_Z/2$.
For $\deg\phi_Z=1$, the curve $\widetilde Z$ is rational.
The exceptional components of $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ are also rational.
Summing the genera of the normalization components of $Y_p$ and
using \eqref{eq:real-explicit-branch-count}, we get $a_p\le2N$.
Together with \eqref{eq:real-explicit-odd-component-node-bound},
this proves (iv).

For the prime two, put $r=(g+1)/4$ and $n=2r-1$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-dyadic-fibres} gives $2n$ rational
normalization components of $Y_2$, $2(n-1)$ nodes, and $2r$
points where two branches have intersection multiplicity two.
Write $\Gamma(Y_2)=(V_Y,\mathcal E_Y,\partial_Y)$, and let
$\mathcal T_2\subseteq\mathcal E_Y=\operatorname{Sing}(Y_2)$
be the set of the $2r$ points where the two branches have
intersection multiplicity two. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-subgraph}
 \Gamma'=(V_Y,\mathcal E_Y\setminus\mathcal T_2,
                   \partial_Y|_{\mathcal E_Y\setminus\mathcal T_2}).
\end{equation}
The local descriptions in Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-dyadic-fibres}
identify $\Gamma'$ with the disjoint union of two copies of the tree
$\Gamma_2$ from \eqref{eq:start-explicit-fibre-graph}.
Denote the vertex sets of the two connected components of $\Gamma'$
by $V_Y^{(0)}$ and $V_Y^{(1)}$.
For each $E_{m-2,c}$, the union of the divisors $D_a$ meets
$E_{m-2,c}$ at two points. The points of $\mathcal T_2$ map to
$\bigcup_{a,c}(D_a\cap E_{m-2,c})$.
The two branches at every $y\in\mathcal T_2$ lie on the two
components mapping to the same $E_{m-2,c}$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-connecting-edges}
 |\partial_Y(y)\cap V_Y^{(0)}|
   =|\partial_Y(y)\cap V_Y^{(1)}|=1
 \qquad(y\in\mathcal T_2).
\end{equation}
Since $|\mathcal T_2|=2r>0$, the graph $\Gamma(Y_2)$ is connected.
Thus $Y_2$ is connected. By \eqref{eq:bg-explicit-graph-betti},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-betti}
 b_1(\Gamma(Y_2))=2(n-1)+2r-2n+1=\frac{g-1}{2}.
\end{equation}

It remains to verify \eqref{eq:real-explicit-stabilizer-hypotheses}.
Recall that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-sign-group}
 H=\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbb Q(x))=\mathbb F_2^{g+1},\qquad
 A=\ker\!\left(\lambda:H\to\mathbb F_2\right),\qquad
 \lambda((a_i)_{i=0}^g)=\sum_{i=0}^g a_i.
\end{equation}
Under the identification in \eqref{eq:real-explicit-sign-group}, the
standard basis vector $e_i\in\mathbb F_2^{g+1}$ represents the field
automorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-basis-automorphism}
 e_i(x)=x,\qquad e_i(z_i)=-z_i,\qquad
 e_i(z_h)=z_h\quad(0\le h\le g,\ h\ne i).
\end{equation}
The morphism $S_{-1}\to X_{-1}$ is an isomorphism after base change
to $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z_{(2)}$. Therefore $(S_0)_{\mathbb Z_{(2)}}$ is the
normalization of $P_2$ in $L$; denote the normalization morphism by
$\psi:(S_0)_{\mathbb Z_{(2)}}\to P_2$.

Let $\bar q\in(P_2)_{\overline{\mathbb F}_2}$ satisfy
$\bar q\notin\bigcup_{a=0}^{N-1}D_a$, and put
$\mathcal A=\mathcal O^{\mathrm{sh}}_{P_2,\bar q}$.
For $0\le i\le g$, the rational function
$A_i=-2^{m-2}/(x-i)$ belongs to $\mathcal A$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-split-equations}
 W_i=\frac{z_i/(x-i)-1}{2},\qquad
 W_i^2+W_i=A_i.
\end{equation}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-split-algebras}
 \mathcal A[w_i]/(w_i^2+w_i-A_i)\simeq\mathcal A\times\mathcal A,
 \qquad
 \bigotimes_{i=0}^g\mathcal A[w_i]/(w_i^2+w_i-A_i)
      \simeq\mathcal A^{\,2^{g+1}}.
\end{equation}
The tensor product in \eqref{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-split-algebras}
is the integral closure of $\mathcal A$ in
$L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal A)$.
Hence the action of $H$ on the geometric points of
$\psi^{-1}(\bar q)$ is simply transitive. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-trivial-stabilizers}
 H_{\bar x}=\{0\},\qquad A_{\bar x}=\{0\}
 \quad\text{for every }\bar x\in\psi^{-1}(\bar q).
\end{equation}

Put $k=m-2$. For $0\le j<2^{m-1}$, write
$j=c+2^ke$, with $0\le c<2^k$ and $e\in\{0,1\}$, and let
$\bar q_j$ be the point of $E_{k,c}$ with
$X_{k,c}=\overline e$.
The divisors $D_a$ containing $\bar q_j$ are exactly those with
$a\equiv j\pmod{2^{m-1}}$.
Let $\mathcal A_j=\mathcal O^{\mathrm{sh}}_{P_2,\bar q_j}$ and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-scaled-roots}
 t=\frac{x-j}{2^{m-2}},\qquad
 U=\frac{z_j}{2^{m-2}},\qquad
 V=\frac{z_{j+2^{m-1}}}{2^{m-2}}.
\end{equation}
For each $i\notin\{j,j+2^{m-1}\}$, the integral closure of
$\mathcal A_j$ in
$\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal A_j)[z_i]/(z_i^2-f_i)$ is
$\mathcal A_j\times\mathcal A_j$, by
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity}.
For the indices $j$ and $j+2^{m-1}$, the equations become
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-pair-equations}
 U^2=t(t-4),\qquad V^2=(t-2)(t-6).
\end{equation}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-regularity}, the integral closure
of $\mathcal A_j$ in
$\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal A_j)[U,V]/(U^2-t(t-4),V^2-(t-2)(t-6))$
is the algebra
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-normalization}
 \mathcal B_j=\mathcal A_j[a,b]/
       (a^2+ta+t,\ b^2+(t-2)b+t-2),
 \qquad U=t+2a,\quad V=t-2+2b.
\end{equation}
The algebra $\mathcal B_j$ is free of rank four over $\mathcal A_j$
and has one closed point. The remaining $g-1$ quadratic factors
split over $\mathcal A_j$. Thus the integral closure of
$\mathcal A_j$ in $L\otimes_{\mathbb Q(x)}\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal A_j)$ is a product
of $2^{g-1}$ copies of $\mathcal B_j$.
For every $\bar x\in\psi^{-1}(\bar q_j)$, the stabilizers are
therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-nontrivial-stabilizers}
 H_{\bar x}=\langle e_j,e_{j+2^{m-1}}\rangle\simeq\mathbb F_2^2,
 \qquad
 A_{\bar x}=H_{\bar x}\cap\ker\lambda
           =\langle e_j+e_{j+2^{m-1}}\rangle\simeq\mathbb F_2.
\end{equation}
Proposition~\ref{prop:starting-dyadic-fibres} gives exactly one
point $y_j\in Y_2$ mapping to $\bar q_j$.
Put $B_2=\{y_j:0\le j<2^{m-1}\}$. The points $\bar q_j$ are
distinct, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:real-explicit-exceptional-point-count}
 |B_2|=2^{m-1}=2r=\frac{g+1}{2}.
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:real-explicit-trivial-stabilizers} and
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-nontrivial-stabilizers} prove
\eqref{eq:real-explicit-stabilizer-hypotheses} for this set $B_2$.
Together with \eqref{eq:real-explicit-dyadic-betti} and the description
of the normalization components and singular points of $Y_2$, this
proves (v).
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-18: body_md -->
\label{prop:good-prime-twist}
Let $\widetilde S\to S_0\to S_{-1}$ be an anchored diagram.  Every central
lift $P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$ has a twist by $C\times_{\mathbb Q}D_d$,
for a positive squarefree integer $d$ whose prime divisors lie outside
$\Sigma$ (with $d=1$ allowed), that extends to a central lift
$P_U\to\widetilde S_U$.  Consequently an extension class has a central lift
over $\mathbb Q$ if and only if it has one over $U$.
<!-- end proposition-18 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
On the trivial torsor $\widetilde C\times\mathbb F_2$, each $e\in E_\theta$
acts by $(z,b)\mapsto(\rho_\theta(e)z,b+\lambda(e))$ for some
$\lambda(e)\in\mathbb F_2$.  The formula $(z,b)\mapsto(\rho_\theta(e)z,b+\lambda(e))$
defines the action of $e$ on $\widetilde S_U\times\mathbb F_2$ as well.  Suppose therefore that
$P_{\mathbb Q}$ is connected, with quadratic function field
$\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)(\sqrt a)$ for
$a\in\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)^\times$.  Let $n_a:N_a\to\widetilde S_U$ be the normalization of
$\widetilde S_U$ in $\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)(\sqrt a)$.
The morphism $n_a$ is finite because $\widetilde S_U$ is excellent.
Let $Z_a\subseteq N_a$ be the closed subset where $n_a$ is not \'etale.
The image of $Z_a$ under the proper morphism $N_a\to U$ is closed.
Since $(N_a)_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$ is the \'etale morphism
$P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$, that image does not contain the generic
point of $U$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-ramification-prime-set}
 \Sigma_a:=\operatorname{im}(Z_a\to U)
 \quad\text{is a finite subset of }\{p:p\notin\Sigma\}.
\end{equation}

For $p\notin\Sigma$, the map $\widetilde S_U\to U$ is smooth, so every
irreducible component of $(\widetilde S_U)_p$ has multiplicity one.
For an irreducible component $D$ of $(\widetilde S_U)_p$, let
$\eta_D$ be the generic point of $D$ and let $v_D$ be the
normalized discrete valuation of the ring
$\mathcal O_{\widetilde S_U,\eta_D}$.  Since $p\ne2$, the extension
$\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)(\sqrt a)/\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)$ ramifies there if
and only if $v_D(a)$ is odd.  The group $G$ acts transitively on the irreducible components of
$(\widetilde S_U)_p$, because $X_p$ is geometrically integral and
$\widetilde S_U\to X$ is a $G$-torsor. The $E_\theta$-action on
$\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)(\sqrt a)$ extends to $N_a$. Choose a point $w\in N_a$ with $n_a(w)=\eta_D$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-divisorial-inertia-definition}
 I_w=\{a\in j_\theta(\mathbb F_2):aw=w,\quad
          a|_{\kappa(w)}=\operatorname{id}_{\kappa(w)}\}.
\end{equation}
The group $j_\theta(\mathbb F_2)$ acts transitively on the points of
$n_a^{-1}(\eta_D)$. Since $j_\theta(\mathbb F_2)$ is abelian, the
subgroup $I_w$ is independent of the choice of $w$; denote that
subgroup by $I_D$. If $e\in E_\theta$ and $g=\rho_\theta(e)$,
then $n_a(ew)=g\eta_D$ and $I_{ew}=eI_we^{-1}$. Centrality
of $j_\theta(\mathbb F_2)$ now gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-inertia-parity}
 I_{gD}=eI_De^{-1}=I_D,\qquad
 v_{gD}(a)\equiv v_D(a)\pmod2.
\end{equation}

For $p\notin\Sigma$, let $\epsilon_p\in\{0,1\}$ be the
common parity in \eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-inertia-parity}. By
\eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-ramification-prime-set}, only finitely many
$\epsilon_p$ are nonzero. Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-constant-twist}
 d=\prod_{\substack{p\notin\Sigma\\\epsilon_p=1}}p.
\end{equation}
For every irreducible component $D$ of $(\widetilde S_U)_p$,
smoothness gives $v_D(p)=1$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-twisted-valuations}
 v_D(da)=v_D(a)+\epsilon_p\equiv0\pmod2.
\end{equation}  At the generic point of every horizontal divisor on $\widetilde S_U$,
$d$ is a unit and the residue characteristic is zero, so adjoining
$\sqrt d$ is also unramified there.  Since
$P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$ was \'etale, the normalization in the
quadratic algebra defined by $z^2=da$ is unramified at every
codimension-one point of the regular surface $\widetilde S_U$.  Purity of the branch locus now shows that the normalization morphism
to $\widetilde S_U$ is \'etale; see \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0BMB}{\textup{[Stacks, 0BMB]}}.

To identify the extension acting on the twist, let $G_{\mathbb Q(C)}$ be
the absolute Galois group of $\mathbb Q(C)$ and let
$\rho:G_{\mathbb Q(C)}\to E_\theta$ be the homomorphism obtained from
$P_{\mathbb Q}\to C$.  Its composite with $\rho_\theta$ is the quotient
corresponding to $\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)/\mathbb Q(C)$.  Let
$\chi_d:G_{\mathbb Q(C)}\to\mathbb F_2$ be the pullback of the character of
$G_\mathbb Q$ classified by $D_d\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$.  Then
$\rho_d(\gamma)=j_\theta(\chi_d(\gamma))\rho(\gamma)$ is a
homomorphism and $\rho_\theta\rho_d=\rho_\theta\rho$, since
$j_\theta(\mathbb F_2)$ is central.  The $E_\theta$-action on
$P_{\mathbb Q}\star(C\times_{\mathbb Q}D_d)$ extends to the normalization of
$\widetilde S_U$ in the algebra of
$P_{\mathbb Q}\star(C\times_{\mathbb Q}D_d)$, by functoriality of normalization.  If the twist $P_{\mathbb Q}\star(C\times_{\mathbb Q}D_d)$ is
disconnected, apply the normalization and purity arguments to each
factor of the associated finite $\mathbb Q(\widetilde C)$-algebra. The
result is a central lift over $U$ in that case as well. By
\eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-integral-realizability} and the complement
argument preceding that equation, a disconnected twist can occur only
when $\theta=0$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proposition-19: body_md -->
\label{prop:local-lift-criterion}
For an anchored diagram and
$V\in\{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q,U,B_p,\operatorname{Spec} K_p\}$, there is a natural linear map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-obstruction-map}
 \alpha_V:H^2(G,\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow H_A^2((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
For $\theta\in H^2(G,\mathbb F_2)$, a central lift
$P_V\to\widetilde S_V$ exists if and only if $\alpha_V(\theta)=0$.  If $\alpha_V(\theta)=0$, the isomorphism classes of central lifts
of $\theta$ form a torsor under $H_A^1((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2)$, acting by quadratic twisting.
The maps $\alpha_V$ and the twisting actions commute with pullback
under every base change $V'\to V$ between schemes in
$\{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q,U,B_p,\operatorname{Spec}K_p\}$.  For
$V=U,\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q,\operatorname{Spec} K_p$, descent along the \'etale $A$-torsor
$q_V:(S_0)_V\to(S_{-1})_V$ identifies the target with
$H^2_{\mathrm{\acute et}}((S_{-1})_V,\mathbb F_2)$.
<!-- end proposition-19 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Recall that $H=\ker(G\to A)$. The map
$r_V:\widetilde S_V\to(S_0)_V$ is an \'etale $H$-torsor.
Pullback along $r_V$ gives an exact equivalence from
$A$-equivariant \'etale sheaves on $(S_0)_V$ to $G$-equivariant
\'etale sheaves on $\widetilde S_V$.  The inverse functor is
$\mathcal F\mapsto(r_{V*}\mathcal F)^H$. To check the assertion,
choose an \'etale morphism $W\to(S_0)_V$ trivializing $r_V$. Then
$\widetilde S_V\times_{(S_0)_V}W\simeq\coprod_{h\in H}W$.
The $H$-equivariance identifies the restrictions of $\mathcal F$ to
the copies of $W$. Descent therefore recovers a sheaf on $(S_0)_V$,
and the quotient action of $G/H=A$ gives the $A$-equivariance.
Exactness can be checked after the same \'etale base change.
For a $G$-equivariant sheaf $\mathcal F$ on $\widetilde S_V$, this
equivalence identifies
$\Gamma(\widetilde S_V,\mathcal F)^G$ with
$\Gamma((S_0)_V,(r_{V*}\mathcal F)^H)^A$.  Passing to derived
functors gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:realizable-fixed-descent}
 H_G^i(\widetilde S_V,\mathbb F_2)\simeq H_A^i((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
The group $H_A^i((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2)$ depends on $q:S_0\to S_{-1}$
and $V$, independently of the choice of $r:\widetilde S\to S_0$.
The scheme $\widetilde S_V$ is connected, because
$\widetilde S_{\mathbb Q}$ is geometrically integral by Proposition~\ref{prop:realizing-surface-properties}.
The five-term sequence of \eqref{eq:realizable-equivariant-sequence} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-five-term-lift-sequence}
 H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(\widetilde S_V,\mathbb F_2)^G
 \xrightarrow{d_2}H^2(G,\mathbb F_2)
 \xrightarrow{\alpha_V}H_A^2((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
Here $\alpha_V$ is the edge map to
$H_G^2(\widetilde S_V,\mathbb F_2)$ followed by
\eqref{eq:realizable-fixed-descent}.  A class in
$H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(\widetilde S_V,\mathbb F_2)^G$ is represented by a quadratic
torsor $P\to\widetilde S_V$ such that each $g\in G$ lifts to an
automorphism of $P$.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-torsor-automorphism-extension}
 E(P)=\{(g,\varphi):g\in G,\ \varphi:P\xrightarrow{\sim}P,
             \ \pi_V\varphi=g\pi_V,\
             \varphi\text{ commutes with the }\mathbb F_2\text{-action}\}.
\end{equation}
Give $E(P)$ the multiplication
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-torsor-extension-multiplication}
 (g,\varphi)(h,\psi)=(gh,\varphi\circ\psi).
\end{equation}
The homomorphism $E(P)\to G$ given by $(g,\varphi)\mapsto g$
is a central extension of $G$ by $\mathbb F_2$: every $g$ has a lift, and an
$\mathbb F_2$-equivariant automorphism of $P$ over the connected scheme
$\widetilde S_V$ is translation by an element of $\mathbb F_2$.
Choose $(g,\widehat g)\in E(P)$ for each $g\in G$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-transgression-cocycle}
 d_2[P]=[c_P],\qquad
 c_P(g,h)=\widehat g\widehat h\widehat{gh}^{-1}\in\mathbb F_2.
\end{equation}  To verify \eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-transgression-cocycle}, choose
an \'etale cover $W_0\to\widetilde S_V$ trivializing $P$, and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-equivariant-trivializing-cover}
 W=\prod_{g\in G,\,\widetilde S_V}g^*W_0.
\end{equation}
Here the product is the fibre product over $\widetilde S_V$.
Permuting the factors by the action of $G$ makes
$W\to\widetilde S_V$ a $G$-equivariant \'etale cover, and the
factor indexed by $1\in G$ shows that $W$ trivializes $P$.  Represent
$P$ by a \v Cech cocycle $z$ on
$W\times_{\widetilde S_V}W$, and choose degree-zero \v Cech
cochains $b_g$ on $W$ with $gz-z=\delta b_g$, where $\delta$ is
the \v Cech differential.  The group-cochain differential gives
$d_2z(g,h)=b_g+gb_h-b_{gh}$, the same translation as
$\widehat g\widehat h\widehat{gh}^{-1}$.  Exactness of
\eqref{eq:obs-explicit-five-term-lift-sequence} therefore identifies
$\ker\alpha_V$ with the classes admitting central lifts.  Torsors are
classified by $H^1$ and represented by finite \'etale schemes; see
\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/03AJ}{\textup{[Stacks, 03AJ]}}, \href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/03RV}{\textup{[Stacks, 03RV]}}.

Let $P_1,P_2\to\widetilde S_V$ be central lifts of the extension
$E_\theta$. Define the quadratic torsor
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-difference-quotient}
 D_{12}:=(P_1\times_{\widetilde S_V}P_2)/\Delta\mathbb F_2.
\end{equation}
The action $e(p_1,p_2)=(ep_1,ep_2)$ induces an action of
$E_\theta$ on $D_{12}$. For $a\in\mathbb F_2$, the element $j_\theta(a)$
acts on $P_1\times_{\widetilde S_V}P_2$ by $(p_1,p_2)\mapsto
(p_1+a,p_2+a)$ and hence acts trivially on $D_{12}$. Thus the
$E_\theta$-action on $D_{12}$ factors through $G$, and descent
along $r_V$ gives an $A$-equivariant quadratic torsor on $(S_0)_V$.
Conversely, pulling back an $A$-equivariant quadratic torsor from
$(S_0)_V$ and twisting $P_1$ gives a central lift of $\theta$.
Write $D=D_{12}$ in the following formula.
There is an $E_\theta$-equivariant isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-difference-inverse}
 \begin{aligned}
 (P_1\times_{\widetilde S_V}D)/\Delta\mathbb F_2&\longrightarrow P_2,\\
 [p,[p_1,p_2]]&\longmapsto p_2+a,\qquad p=p_1+a.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The equality $p=p_1+a$ determines $a\in\mathbb F_2$ uniquely.
Replacing $(p_1,p_2)$ by $(p_1+b,p_2+b)$ replaces $a$ by $a+b$,
so $p_2+a$ is unchanged. Replacing $(p,[p_1,p_2])$ by
$(p+b,[p_1+b,p_2])$ also leaves $p_2+a$ unchanged. Thus
\eqref{eq:obs-explicit-difference-inverse} is well defined.
After an \'etale base change trivializing $P_1$ and $P_2$, the map
in \eqref{eq:obs-explicit-difference-inverse} is the addition map
on the copies of $\mathbb F_2$, and is an isomorphism. The same formula,
with a $G$-equivariant torsor $D'\to\widetilde S_V$ in place of
$D_{12}$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-twist-difference-identities}
 P_1\star D_{12}\simeq P_2,\qquad
 (P_1\times_{\widetilde S_V}(P_1\star D'))/\Delta\mathbb F_2\simeq D'.
\end{equation}
Here $P_1\star D'$ denotes the contracted product over
$\widetilde S_V$. Hence twisting acts freely and transitively on the isomorphism
classes of central lifts of $\theta$. The isomorphism
\eqref{eq:realizable-fixed-descent} identifies the group of twists
with $H_A^1((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2)$, proving the assertion.  For $V=U,\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q,\operatorname{Spec} K_p$,
descent along $q_V$ identifies
$H_A^i((S_0)_V,\mathbb F_2)$ with $H^i_{\mathrm{\acute et}}((S_{-1})_V,\mathbb F_2)$.
Pullback under a base change among the schemes $V$ commutes with
$r_V$-descent, the transgression and contracted products, proving the
restriction assertions.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-20: body_md -->
\label{prop:good-detection}
Let $\Sigma$ be a finite set of rational primes containing two, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-detection-bases}
 U=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z[1/\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p],
 \qquad K_p=\operatorname{Frac}(\mathbb Z_{(p)}^h).
\end{equation}
Let $f:X\to U$ be a smooth proper curve with geometrically connected
fibres and a section $s:U\to X$.  Suppose that
$R^1f_*\mathbb F_2$ is constant.  Write $C=X_{\mathbb Q}$.
Then restriction is injective:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-detection-restriction}
 H^2_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(X,\mathbb F_2)
 \longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}
 H^2_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(C_{K_p},\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
The same assertion holds with $K_p$ replaced by $\mathbb Q_p$.
<!-- end proposition-20 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
We first prove that
$H^i_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(U,\mathbb F_2)\to
\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}H^i_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q_p,\mathbb F_2)$
is injective for $i=1,2$.  In this proof all scheme cohomology is
\'etale cohomology, and cohomology of a field means that of its spectrum.  Since $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$, Kummer theory
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-base-kummer-classes}
 H^1(U,\mathbb F_2)
   =\mathbb Z[1/\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p]^\times/
     \bigl(\mathbb Z[1/\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p]^\times\bigr)^2.
\end{equation}
Let $\eta\in H^1(U,\mathbb F_2)$. By
\eqref{eq:obs-explicit-base-kummer-classes}, there are exponents
$\epsilon,\epsilon_p\in\{0,1\}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-kummer-representative}
 \eta=[a],\qquad a=(-1)^\epsilon\prod_{p\in\Sigma}p^{\epsilon_p}.
\end{equation}
Suppose that $a\in\mathbb Q_p^{\times2}$ for every $p\in\Sigma$.
For each $p\in\Sigma$, the normalized $p$-adic valuation gives
$\epsilon_p=v_p(a)\in2\mathbb Z$, so $\epsilon_p=0$.
It follows that $a=(-1)^\epsilon$. An odd $2$-adic square is
congruent to $1$ modulo $8$, whereas $-1\equiv7\pmod8$.
Thus $\epsilon=0$ and $\eta=0$. We have proved that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:base-degree-one-detection}
 H^1(U,\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}H^1(\mathbb Q_p,\mathbb F_2)
 \quad\text{is injective}.
\end{equation}
Taking a basis of a finite-dimensional $\mathbb F_2$-vector space
$M$ and applying \eqref{eq:base-degree-one-detection} to each basis
coordinate proves that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-constant-coefficient-detection}
 H^1(U,M)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}H^1(\mathbb Q_p,M)
 \quad\text{is injective}
\end{equation}
when $M$ denotes the constant sheaf.

Kummer theory and $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$ identify
$H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)$ with $H^2(U,\mathbb G_m)[2]$.
We next prove that the restriction homomorphism
$H^2(U,\mathbb G_m)\to H^2(\mathbb Q,\mathbb G_m)$ is injective.
Consider the valuation sequence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-valuation-sequence}
 0\longrightarrow\mathbb G_{m,U}\longrightarrow
 j_*\mathbb G_{m,\mathbb Q}\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{q\notin\Sigma}i_{q*}\mathbb Z\longrightarrow0,
\end{equation}
where $j:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q\to U$ is the generic inclusion and
$i_q:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb F_q\to U$ is the closed immersion at $q$.
Here
$H^1(U,\bigoplus_{q\notin\Sigma}i_{q*}\mathbb Z)=0$:
$H^1(\mathbb F_q,\mathbb Z)=0$, finite direct image is exact, and
\'etale cohomology on the quasi-compact quasi-separated scheme $U$
commutes with filtered colimits of abelian sheaves.  The long exact cohomology sequence of
\eqref{eq:obs-explicit-valuation-sequence} therefore gives the first
injection below. The stalk formula and Hilbert 90 give
$R^1j_*\mathbb G_m=0$, so the Leray spectral sequence for $j$
gives the second injection:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-brauer-restriction-injections}
 H^2(U,\mathbb G_m)\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
 H^2(U,j_*\mathbb G_{m,\mathbb Q})
 \lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
 H^2(\mathbb Q,\mathbb G_m)=\operatorname{Br}(\mathbb Q).
\end{equation}

Let $\xi\in H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)$.  For $q\notin\Sigma$, restriction factors as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-unramified-local-vanishing}
 H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow H^2(\mathbb Z_q,\mathbb F_2)=0
 \longrightarrow H^2(\mathbb Q_q,\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
The equality in \eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-unramified-local-vanishing}
follows by descent from the strict henselization of $\mathbb Z_q$:
a strictly henselian local scheme has no higher \'etale cohomology,
and $\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb F}_q/\mathbb F_q)$
has torsion cohomological dimension one.
Suppose also that $\xi|_{\mathbb Q_p}=0$ for every $p\in\Sigma$.
Let $\beta\in\operatorname{Br}(\mathbb Q)[2]$ be the image of
$\xi$ under \eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-brauer-restriction-injections}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-unramified-local-vanishing} gives
$\operatorname{inv}_q(\beta|_{\mathbb Q_q})=0$ for every finite
prime $q$. The reciprocity law for Brauer invariants gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-real-invariant}
 \operatorname{inv}_{\infty}(\beta|_{\mathbb R})
 =-\sum_{q\text{ prime}}\operatorname{inv}_q(\beta|_{\mathbb Q_q})=0.
\end{equation}
Brauer--Hasse--Noether therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:base-degree-two-detection}
 H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}H^2(\mathbb Q_p,\mathbb F_2)
 \quad\text{is injective}.
\end{equation}
See \cite[Chapter~VIII, Theorem~4.2]{MilneCFT}.

Apply the Leray spectral sequence (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/03QC}{\textup{[Stacks, 03QC]}})
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-leray-sequence}
 E_2^{a,b}=H^a(U,R^bf_*\mathbb F_2)
 \ \Longrightarrow\ H^{a+b}(X,\mathbb F_2),
\end{equation}
and denote the induced filtration on $H^2(X,\mathbb F_2)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-leray-filtration}
 H^2(X,\mathbb F_2)=F^0\supseteq F^1\supseteq F^2\supseteq0.
\end{equation}
Proper smooth base change identifies $R^0f_*\mathbb F_2$ and
$R^2f_*\mathbb F_2$ with the constant rank-one sheaf.  For $R^2f_*\mathbb F_2$, local constancy follows from
\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/0GLI}{\textup{[Stacks, 0GLI]}}; a rank-one $\mathbb F_2$-local system is constant because
$\operatorname{GL}_1(\mathbb F_2)=\{1\}$.
The equality $s^*\circ f^*=\operatorname{id}_{H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)}$
shows that $f^*:H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)\to H^2(X,\mathbb F_2)$ is
injective.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-leray-base-piece}
 F^2=f^*H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)
       \simeq H^2(U,\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
Thus the differential
$d_2^{0,1}:H^0(U,R^1f_*\mathbb F_2)\to H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)$
is zero.
There are no incoming differentials to the positions $(0,2)$ and $(1,1)$
on pages $r\geq2$.  Consequently the quotients $F^0/F^1$ and $F^1/F^2$ have natural
injections
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-leray-graded-injections}
 F^0/F^1=E_\infty^{0,2}
     \lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow H^0(U,R^2f_*\mathbb F_2),
 \qquad
 F^1/F^2=E_\infty^{1,1}
     \lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow H^1(U,R^1f_*\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
For $f_p:C_{\mathbb Q_p}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q_p$, the same
argument gives a filtration $F_p^\bullet$ of
$H^2(C_{\mathbb Q_p},\mathbb F_2)$ and injections of
$F_p^0/F_p^1$ and $F_p^1/F_p^2$ into
$H^0(\mathbb Q_p,R^2f_{p*}\mathbb F_2)$ and
$H^1(\mathbb Q_p,R^1f_{p*}\mathbb F_2)$, respectively.
Proper base change (\href{https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/095T}{\textup{[Stacks, 095T]}}) identifies $R^bf_{p*}\mathbb F_2$
with the pullback of $R^bf_*\mathbb F_2$, so the restriction map
$H^2(X,\mathbb F_2)\to H^2(C_{\mathbb Q_p},\mathbb F_2)$
takes $F^i$ into $F_p^i$. The induced maps on $F^0/F^1$ and
$F^1/F^2$ commute with the injections in
\eqref{eq:obs-explicit-leray-graded-injections} and the corresponding
injections for $f_p$.

Let $\alpha\in H^2(X,\mathbb F_2)$ restrict to zero in
$H^2(C_{\mathbb Q_p},\mathbb F_2)$ for every $p\in\Sigma$.
The image of $\alpha$ in $F^0/F^1$ is zero, because
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-geometric-degree-two-restriction}
 H^0(U,R^2f_*\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow
 H^0(\mathbb Q_p,R^2f_{p*}\mathbb F_2)
\end{equation}
is the identity on $\mathbb F_2$.  Hence $\alpha\in F^1$.
The image of $\alpha$ in
$F^1/F^2\subseteq H^1(U,R^1f_*\mathbb F_2)$ restricts to zero in
$H^1(\mathbb Q_p,R^1f_{p*}\mathbb F_2)$ for every $p\in\Sigma$.
Apply \eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-constant-coefficient-detection} with
$M=H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(C_{\overline\mathbb Q},\mathbb F_2)$, using the assumed
constancy of $R^1f_*\mathbb F_2$. The image of $\alpha$ in $F^1/F^2$
is zero, so $\alpha\in F^2$.  By \eqref{eq:obs-explicit-leray-base-piece},
$\alpha=f^*\beta$ for a unique
$\beta\in H^2(U,\mathbb F_2)$.  For the base-changed section
$s_p:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q_p\to C_{\mathbb Q_p}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-section-detects-base-class}
 \beta|_{\mathbb Q_p}
 =s_p^*f_p^*(\beta|_{\mathbb Q_p})
 =s_p^*(\alpha|_{C_{\mathbb Q_p}})=0.
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:obs-explicit-section-detects-base-class} and
\eqref{eq:base-degree-two-detection} give $\beta=0$ and hence $\alpha=f^*\beta=0$. This proves
injectivity of \eqref{eq:obs-explicit-detection-restriction} with
$\mathbb Q_p$ in place of $K_p$.

Finally, the map $\mathbb Z_{(p)}\to\mathbb Z_p$ extends to the
henselization, since $\mathbb Z_p$ is henselian. The extended local homomorphism
$\mathbb Z_{(p)}^h\to\mathbb Z_p$ is injective: a nonzero kernel
in the discrete valuation ring $\mathbb Z_{(p)}^h$ would contain a
power of $p$, whereas every power of $p$ has nonzero image in
$\mathbb Z_p$. We obtain embeddings $K_p\hookrightarrow\mathbb Q_p$
and factorizations
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-henselian-completed-restriction}
 H^2(X,\mathbb F_2)\longrightarrow H^2(C_{K_p},\mathbb F_2)
 \longrightarrow H^2(C_{\mathbb Q_p},\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
Injectivity after restriction to all $C_{\mathbb Q_p}$ therefore
implies \eqref{eq:obs-explicit-detection-restriction}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition-21: body_md -->
\label{prop:geometric-twist-obstruction}
For an anchored diagram, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-rational-integral-kernels}
 \mathscr R=\ker\bigl((\rho_p\alpha_p)_p\bigr),\qquad
 \mathscr L=\ker\bigl((\alpha_p)_p\bigr)
 \quad\text{inside }H^2(G,\mathbb F_2).
\end{equation}
Then $\mathscr R$ is the space of extension classes admitting a central
lift $P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$, and $\mathscr L$ is the space
admitting a central lift $P_p\to\widetilde S_{B_p}$ for every
$p\in\Sigma$.
There is a canonical linear map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-compatibility-map}
 \delta:\mathscr L\longrightarrow\mathcal D_\Sigma,
 \qquad \ker\delta=\mathscr E(\widetilde S,S_{-1}).
\end{equation}
In particular
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-obstruction-filtration}
 H^2(G,\mathbb F_2)\supseteq\mathscr R\supseteq\mathscr L
       \supseteq\mathscr E(\widetilde S,S_{-1}),
\end{equation}
and the successive quotients inject, respectively, into
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-quotient-targets}
 \bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}\operatorname{im}\rho_p,
 \qquad\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}\ker\rho_p,
 \qquad\mathcal D_\Sigma.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bad-only-obstruction-budget}
 \dim_{\mathbb F_2}\mathcal O(\widetilde S,S_{-1})
 \le\sum_{p\in\Sigma}\dim_{\mathbb F_2}\mathcal B_p^2
       +\dim_{\mathbb F_2}\mathcal D_\Sigma.
\end{equation}
The groups $\mathcal B_p^2$ and $\mathcal D_\Sigma$ depend only
on $q:S_0\to S_{-1}$ and $\Sigma$, independently of
$r:\widetilde S\to S_0$.
<!-- end proposition-21 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
If $\theta$ admits a central lift $P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$,
the morphism $(P_{\mathbb Q})_{K_p}\to\widetilde C_{K_p}$ is a central lift, so
$\rho_p\alpha_p(\theta)=\alpha_{\operatorname{Spec} K_p}(\theta)=0$.
Conversely, if $\rho_p\alpha_p(\theta)=0$ for every $p\in\Sigma$,
then $\alpha_U(\theta)\in H^2_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(X,\mathbb F_2)$ restricts to zero in
each $H^2_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(C_{K_p},\mathbb F_2)$.  Proposition~\ref{prop:good-detection}
implies that $\alpha_U(\theta)=0$.  By
Proposition~\ref{prop:local-lift-criterion}, there is then a central lift
$P_U\to\widetilde S_U$, whose generic fibre is a central lift
$P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$.  This proves the assertion about
$\mathscr R$. Moreover, Proposition~\ref{prop:good-prime-twist}
shows that, for every central lift $P_{\mathbb Q}\to\widetilde C$, there
is a squarefree $d>0$ such that
$P_{\mathbb Q}\star(C\times_{\mathbb Q}D_d)$ extends to a central lift over $U$.
By the definition of $\mathscr R$, we obtain the injection
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-rational-obstruction-injection}
 H^2(G,\mathbb F_2)/\mathscr R\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}\operatorname{im}\rho_p,\qquad
 [\theta]\longmapsto(\rho_p\alpha_p(\theta))_{p\in\Sigma}.
\end{equation}

For $\theta\in\mathscr R$ and $p\in\Sigma$, the definition
of $\mathscr R$ gives $\alpha_p(\theta)\in\ker\rho_p$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:local-lift-criterion} gives the equivalences
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-integral-local-obstruction}
 \theta\in\mathscr L
 \ \Longleftrightarrow\ 
 \alpha_p(\theta)=0\text{ for every }p\in\Sigma
 \ \Longleftrightarrow\ 
 \text{a central lift over }B_p\text{ exists for every }p\in\Sigma.
\end{equation}
Consequently we obtain the injection
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-local-integral-injection}
 \mathscr R/\mathscr L\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma}\ker\rho_p,\qquad
 [\theta]\longmapsto(\alpha_p(\theta))_{p\in\Sigma}.
\end{equation}

Let $\theta\in\mathscr L$. Choose central lifts
$P_U\to\widetilde S_U$ and $P_p\to\widetilde S_{B_p}$ for each
$p\in\Sigma$. Define the following torsor on $\widetilde C_{K_p}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-explicit-local-difference-quotient}
 D_p:=\bigl((P_U)_{K_p}\times_{\widetilde C_{K_p}}(P_p)_{K_p}\bigr)
       /\Delta\mathbb F_2.
\end{equation}
As in Proposition~\ref{prop:local-lift-criterion}, the diagonal
$E_\theta$-action on $D_p$ factors through $G$. Descent along
the $G$-torsor $\widetilde C_{K_p}\to C_{K_p}$ therefore gives
a quadratic torsor $\overline D_p\to C_{K_p}$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-delta-definition}
 \chi_p=[\overline D_p]\in Q_p,\qquad
 \delta(\theta)=[(\chi_p)_{p\in\Sigma}]\in\mathcal D_\Sigma.
\end{equation}
Twisting $P_U$ by $\eta\in H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(X,\mathbb F_2)$ and $P_p$ by
$\eta_p\in\mathcal B_p^1$ replaces $\chi_p$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-mechanical-choice-change}
 \chi_p+\eta|_{C_{K_p}}+\operatorname{res}_p^1(\eta_p).
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:twist-defect-group}, the tuples in
\eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-choice-change} and
\eqref{eq:obs-mechanical-delta-definition} have the same image in
